  - [18:44:48 - 18:46:19] **Requirements for Valid Actual Consent**

    Let me now focus on the first method to comply with the consent obligation: actively getting actual consent. What exactly do you need to do to ensure this actual consent is valid? Basically, there are two strict requirements. First, you must ensure that the individual is fully aware of your specific purposes for the collection, use, and disclosure of their data. As you will see later, this is inextricably linked to the notification obligation, which is the second major obligation we are covering today. For your actual consent to be legally valid, you must explicitly tell the individual, "Here is exactly what I want to do with your personal data," and then the individual must give their consent strictly for those specified purposes. That is the fundamental mechanic of how consent works; it is a purpose-based regime. Obtaining actual consent is generally a very straightforward process as long as you clearly articulate your purposes and the individual agrees to them. However, there are specific ways organizations attempt to get actual consent that render it completely invalid. There are two main practices that invalidate actual consent, the first of which I classify as "unreasonable tying."  

    现在让我集中讲讲履行同意义务的第一种方法：主动获取实际同意。你究竟需要做什么来确保这种实际同意是有效的？基本上，有两个严格的要求。首先，你必须确保个人完全了解你收集、使用和披露其数据的具体目的。大家稍后会看到，这与通知义务密不可分，这也是我们今天涵盖的第二个主要义务。为了使你的实际同意在法律上有效，你必须明确告诉个人：“这正是我打算如何使用你的个人数据”，然后个人必须严格针对这些指定目的给予同意。这就是同意运作的基本机制；它是一个基于目的的制度。只要你清楚地阐明你的目的并且个人同意，获得实际同意通常是一个非常直接的过程。然而，组织在尝试获取实际同意时，如果采用某些特定方式，会使其完全无效。主要有两种做法会使实际同意无效，我将第一种归类为“不合理的捆绑”（Unreasonable Tying）。  

  - [18:46:19 - 18:52:39] **Invalidating Consent: Unreasonable Tying**

    Unreasonable tying occurs when you, as an organization providing a product or service, tell an individual that unless they consent to the collection and use of their data, you will refuse to provide the product or service—even when that data is not reasonably necessary to provide the service. A student brought up the example of standard websites and cookie consent banners. The professor agreed, noting that when you visit a website, a pop-up often appears asking for personal data to be used in specific ways. Standard websites typically separate these requests into different checkboxes: one for "essential data" or "essential cookies" required for the site to function, and others for unnecessary, optional tracking. Demanding consent for essential cookies is perfectly fine. However, the situation gets complicated with big tech companies like Facebook. In Europe, there have been massive legal proceedings regarding this. Facebook argues that targeted advertising requires consent, but they force that consent by claiming the advertising revenue is technically necessary to fund and provide the "free" service itself. They argue that without monetizing the data, the service couldn't exist. The professor expressed doubt that this argument would fly with the Personal Data Protection Commission (PDPC) in Singapore, though acknowledging the common-sense business reality that free services must make money somehow. It remains controversial. But beyond these edge cases, unreasonable tying clearly invalidates consent. The professor then provided a concrete example: Sarah wants to sign up for a spa package. The spa's terms and conditions state that they will share and sell her personal data to third-party marketing agencies. Naturally, Sarah doesn't want this and asks the spa not to disclose her data to third parties. The spa refuses, stating she must agree to all terms to receive the service. Sarah is left with a binary choice: accept the sale of her data or get no service at all. This is a textbook case of unreasonableness because the spa absolutely does not need to sell Sarah's personal data just to provide her with a facial or massage. Even if Sarah feels she has no choice and signs the form, her consent is legally invalid, and she can file a successful complaint with the PDPC. To do this correctly, the spa should separate the consent: require what is necessary to provide the service, and make the third-party marketing disclosure an optional checkbox. If Sarah then actively checks the optional box, the consent is valid because it wasn't forced.  

    当您作为一个提供产品或服务的组织，告诉某人除非他们同意收集和使用他们的数据，否则您将拒绝提供产品或服务时，就会发生不合理的捆绑——即使这些数据对于提供服务并非合理必要。一位学生提出了标准网站和 Cookie 同意横幅的例子。教授表示赞同，指出当你访问一个网站时，经常会弹出一个窗口，要求以特定方式使用个人数据。标准网站通常将这些请求分为不同的复选框：一个用于网站运行所需的“基本数据”或“基本 Cookie”，其他用于不必要的、可选的跟踪。要求对基本 Cookie 给予同意是完全没问题的。然而，对于像 Facebook 这样的大型科技公司来说，情况就变得复杂了。在欧洲，已经有大量关于此事的法律诉讼。Facebook 辩称定向广告需要用户的同意，但他们通过声称广告收入在技术上是资助和提供“免费”服务本身所必需的，来强迫用户给予这种同意。他们认为，如果不将数据货币化，这项服务就不可能存在。教授表示怀疑这种论点能否在新加坡个人数据保护委员会（PDPC）那里行得通，尽管他也承认免费服务必须以某种方式赚钱这一常识性的商业现实。这仍然存在争议。但在这些边缘案例之外，不合理的捆绑显然会使同意无效。教授随后提供了一个具体的例子：莎拉想报名参加一个水疗套餐。水疗中心的条款和条件规定，他们将与第三方营销机构共享并出售她的个人数据。很自然地，莎拉不希望这样，并要求水疗中心不要向第三方披露她的数据。水疗中心拒绝了，表示她必须同意所有条款才能接受服务。莎拉面临着非此即彼的选择：要么接受她的数据被出售，要么根本得不到任何服务。这是一个典型的不合理案例，因为水疗中心绝对不需要为了给莎拉提供面部护理或按摩服务而出售她的个人数据。即使莎拉觉得别无选择而签署了表格，她的同意在法律上也是无效的，她可以向 PDPC 提出成功的投诉。为了正确地做到这一点，水疗中心应该将同意分开：要求提供服务所必需的内容，并将第三方营销披露作为一个可选的复选框。如果莎拉随后主动勾选了可选框，那么该同意就是有效的，因为它不是被强迫的。  

  - [18:52:39 - 18:56:42] **Compelling Consent and Government Mandates**

    The professor then asked the class why the law regards this unrelated tying as unreasonable. A student suggested it was because the data wasn't required for the core transaction. The professor agreed, explaining that the fundamental issue is the element of force. If an organization forces an individual to say "yes" to data collection that isn't necessary for the product, that "yes" loses its meaning; it is not freely given. However, to be absolutely clear, organizations *can* sometimes force an individual to give consent, provided the data is reasonably required to deliver the product or service. For instance, if an individual signs up for a mobile plan with a telecom service provider, the telecom company can absolutely require the customer to agree to the collection of their name, address, and location data. This is valid because the telecom provider genuinely needs this personal data to route calls and bill the customer. A student then asked about government mandates, raising the example of needing to show a passport to obtain a phone number. The student argued this isn't strictly necessary for the technical service, but the government requires it for identity control. The professor provided two ways to view this: one could argue the data *is* necessary for the organization to provide the service because they are legally obliged to verify identity, or, more straightforwardly, government legal requirements override the PDPA. In that scenario, the organization doesn't even need to rely on PDPA consent rules because a higher statutory law mandates the collection. Interestingly, the PDPC guidelines concede another scenario where organizations can compel consent: when they provide a direct incentive or quid pro quo in exchange for it. For example, if a company offers a 20% discount or entry into a lucky draw specifically in exchange for you signing up for email marketing, it is fair for the organization to demand that consent. Essentially, the organization is explicitly purchasing your consent through a tangible reward.  

    教授随后问全班同学，为什么法律将这种无关的捆绑视为不合理。一名学生表示，这是因为核心交易并不需要这些数据。教授表示赞同，解释说根本问题在于强迫的成分。如果一个组织强迫个人对产品非必要的数据收集说“是”，那么这个“是”就失去了意义；它不是自由给予的。然而，需要绝对明确的是，组织有时*确实可以*强迫个人给予同意，前提是这些数据是交付产品或服务所合理要求的。例如，如果一个人与电信服务提供商签约了一个移动套餐，电信公司绝对可以要求客户同意收集他们的姓名、地址和位置数据。这是有效的，因为电信提供商确实需要这些个人数据来路由呼叫和向客户计费。随后有学生问到政府规定的情况，举了需要出示护照才能获得电话号码的例子。该学生认为，这对于技术服务而言并非绝对必要，但政府出于身份控制的需要要求这样做。教授提供了两种看待这个问题的方式：一种观点认为，这些数据对于组织提供服务*是*必要的，因为他们有法律义务核实身份；或者，更直接地说，政府的法律要求凌驾于 PDPA 之上。在这种情况下，组织甚至不需要依赖 PDPA 的同意规则，因为有更高级别的成文法强制要求收集。有趣的是，PDPC 指南承认了组织可以强制要求同意的另一种情况：当他们提供直接的激励或交换条件时。例如，如果一家公司提供 20% 的折扣或参加抽奖的机会，专门作为你注册电子邮件营销的交换条件，那么该组织要求你给予同意是公平的。本质上，该组织是通过有形的奖励明确地购买你的同意。  

  - [18:56:42 - 19:01:33] **Complexities of Discounts, Penalties, and the Deception Element**

    A student asked a practical question: what happens if you sign up for an application that forces you to share your data with 30 participating partner companies to get the service? Similarly, what if an exhibition forces you to share your personal data with all its exhibitors to gain entry? The professor replied that for the exhibition, it is hard to argue that sharing data with third-party exhibitors is strictly necessary just to grant physical entry to the hall. Unless there is a highly specific, functional reason why exhibitors must have your data, forcing this consent would likely be invalid. Another student then expanded on the earlier spa example, asking if it becomes valid if the spa offers a 20% discount in exchange for the data sharing. The professor confirmed that this is valid, as it represents a clear quid pro quo incentive. But the student smartly followed up: what if the spa sets a high base price, and tells the customer they must pay a 30% "penalty" surcharge if they refuse to share their data? The professor noted this is exactly the tricky follow-up question he anticipated. The concept of a discount becomes legally murky here. Is it a genuine discount rewarding the customer, or is it the standard price, making the "non-discounted" price a coercive penalty for protecting one's privacy? In such grey areas, the PDPC or the regulator would have to investigate the business's pricing history to determine if it’s a real discount or a punitive measure designed to force consent. Another student pointed out that websites often ask for consent for "necessary information," but consumers rarely know what "necessary" actually entails, asking if there is a way to find out. The professor clarified that while other jurisdictions might require strict upfront declarations of exactly what data falls under that umbrella, Singapore's regime currently does not strictly require such detailed upfront declarations. However, standard website consent boxes usually have a link to a privacy policy where diligent users can figure out exactly what data is being collected. Concluding the "unreasonable tying" point, the professor reiterated the best practice: differentiate between necessary and optional purposes, and allow individuals to opt out of the optional ones. Moving on, the second way to invalidate consent is through deception. Straightforwardly lying to an individual is an obvious invalidation. However, the PDPC guidelines also include deceptive practices: using "weasel words" (vague, confusing, or fake terms), printing the purposes in illegible, tiny fonts, or hiding the consent terms in an obscure, hard-to-find location. All these practices legally invalidate the consent.  

    有学生问了一个实际问题：如果你注册一个应用程序，它强迫你与30家参与的合作公司分享你的数据才能获得服务，该怎么办？同样，如果一个展览强迫你与所有参展商分享你的个人数据才能入场，情况又会怎样？教授回答说，对于展览而言，很难证明为了获得进入展厅的物理许可，就绝对有必要与第三方参展商分享数据。除非参展商必须拥有你的数据有其非常具体的、功能性的原因，否则强迫给予这种同意很可能是无效的。另一名学生随后对早先的水疗中心例子进行了引申，问如果水疗中心提供20%的折扣作为分享数据的交换条件，这是否就有效了。教授证实这确实有效，因为它代表了一种明确的交换激励。但这名学生机智地追问：如果水疗中心设定了一个很高的基础价格，并告诉顾客如果拒绝分享数据就必须支付30%的“罚款”附加费呢？教授指出，这正是他预料到的棘手的后续问题。在这里，折扣的概念在法律上变得模糊不清。这到底是对顾客的真正折扣奖励，还是标准价格，从而使得“未打折”的价格成为一种惩罚，用来胁迫那些想要保护隐私的人？在这种灰色地带，PDPC或监管机构将不得不调查该企业的定价历史，以确定它是真正的折扣，还是旨在强迫同意的惩罚措施。另一位学生指出，网站经常要求用户同意收集“必要信息”，但消费者很少知道“必要”到底包含什么，他问是否有办法查明。教授澄清说，虽然其他司法管辖区可能要求在前期严格声明到底哪些数据属于这个范畴，但新加坡目前的制度并不严格要求如此详细的前期声明。然而，标准的网站同意框通常会有一个指向隐私政策的链接，细心的用户可以在那里弄清楚到底在收集哪些数据。在总结“不合理的捆绑”这一点时，教授重申了最佳实践：区分必要和可选的目的，并允许个人选择退出可选目的。接下来，使同意无效的第二种方式是欺骗。对个人直截了当的撒谎显然会使其无效。然而，PDPC指南还包括欺骗性做法：使用“狡辩之词”（模糊、令人困惑或虚假的术语），用难以辨认的微小字体打印目的，或将同意条款隐藏在一个不起眼、难以找到的位置。所有这些做法都会在法律上使同意无效。  

  - [19:03:29 - 19:08:14] **Consent from Minors and Representatives**

    Another significant problem, particularly in online settings, is attempting to obtain consent from a minor. Consent given by a young person is generally invalid because the legal understanding is that children lack the capacity to comprehend what they are consenting to. A key case demonstrating this is the Spring College International case. This organization, a private educational institution, used its Facebook page for marketing. The complainant was the parent of a student. While browsing Facebook, the parent found a post on the school's page featuring a picture of her son, proclaiming that this child they taught had successfully gained admission to a specific secondary school. The promotional post contained detailed personal data, including the child's full name, date of birth, primary school, and how long he had studied with the organization. The parent complained to the PDPC. Upon investigation, the PDPC found that the organization had breached the law by showcasing minors (children below the age of 13) without obtaining any valid consent. In this specific case, the school didn't even attempt to get consent. But it raises the question: what if they had tried to get consent directly from the child? The PDPC has discussed the general principle organizations must follow when dealing with minors: they must carefully consider whether the person has sufficient understanding of the nature and consequences of giving consent. If you are dealing with someone under 21, you have to assess their maturity. However, since this is vague, the PDPC provides a practical rule of thumb: anyone who is at least 13 years old is typically considered capable of giving consent for standard matters. But this cannot be blindly assumed. If the data processing is complex or potentially harmful in the future, even a 13-year-old's consent might be scrutinized. A student asked how to handle situations where a minor's consent is invalid, asking if a guardian can give it instead. The professor confirmed this, introducing the concept of getting consent from a representative. For a child, this would be a parent or legal guardian. However, if an organization relies on a representative, they are required to conduct a reasonable measure of due diligence to verify that this person is actually the parent or guardian of that specific child. For maximum safety, organizations can consult both the child and the parent to ensure all bases are covered.  

    另一个重大问题，特别是在网络环境中，是试图获取未成年人的同意。年轻人给予的同意通常是无效的，因为法律认为儿童缺乏理解他们所同意事项的能力。证明这一点的一个关键案例是春天国际学院（Spring College International）案。该组织是一家私立教育机构，利用其Facebook页面进行营销。投诉人是一名学生的家长。在浏览Facebook时，该家长在学校的页面上发现了一篇帖子，上面有她儿子的照片，宣称这个他们教过的孩子已成功获得某中学的录取。这篇宣传帖包含了详细的个人数据，包括孩子的全名、出生日期、小学以及他在该机构学习了多长时间。家长向PDPC投诉。经调查，PDPC发现该组织在未获得任何有效同意的情况下展示未成年人（13岁以下的儿童），违反了法律。在这个具体案例中，学校甚至没有尝试去获取同意。但这引出了一个问题：如果他们试图直接从孩子那里获取同意会怎样呢？PDPC讨论了组织在与未成年人打交道时必须遵循的一般原则：他们必须仔细考虑该人是否有足够的理解力去明白给予同意的性质和后果。如果你处理的是21岁以下的人，你必须评估他们的成熟度。然而，由于这比较模糊，PDPC提供了一个实用的经验法则：任何年满13岁的人通常被认为有能力对一般事项给予同意。但这不能盲目假设。如果数据处理很复杂或在未来有潜在的危害，即使是13岁孩子的同意也可能会受到审查。有学生问如何处理未成年人同意无效的情况，询问是否可以由监护人代为给予。教授证实了这一点，介绍了从代表那里获取同意的概念。对于儿童来说，代表就是父母或法定监护人。然而，如果组织依赖代表，他们需要进行合理程度的尽职调查，以核实此人确实是该特定儿童的父母或监护人。为了最大程度的安全，组织可以同时咨询孩子和家长，以确保万无一失。  

  - [19:08:14 - 19:15:43] **The Form of Consent: Verbal vs. Written**

    Moving to the second part of the lesson, we look at the form of consent—the actual method used to obtain it. The PDPA does not strictly specify any mandatory form, meaning in principle, you can get consent in any form at any time. However, organizations are expected to comply reasonably, meaning the form must be appropriate to the situation. Consent can be either in writing or verbal. For instance, if an individual calls a service provider to sign up for services over the phone, the provider can ask, "Can we get your consent to use your personal data to provide these services?" If the caller says yes, that is valid verbal consent. However, the PDPC clearly states it is good practice to follow up and confirm that verbal consent in writing. The professor asked the class why this is a good practice. Multiple students answered that it is primarily a question of proof. Verbal consent is notoriously difficult to prove later unless the entire phone call was perfectly recorded and archived. Having a written document or declaration form clearly shows exactly what specific details were consented to, resolving disputes if the customer later claims they didn't agree to a specific clause. Furthermore, a written record captures the exact date and time the consent was given. Another student brought up a personal scenario regarding calling banks or airlines, where an automated voice says the call is being recorded. The student wondered if providing personal details to verify identity on these calls constitutes giving verbal consent. The professor clarified that if you are calling a bank where you already have an account, the bank isn't seeking new PDPA consent because they already obtained all necessary consent when you initially signed the comprehensive account opening forms. A different student mentioned insurance agents who call and get verbal consent but then offer a seven-day cooling-off period where the customer can withdraw. The professor noted that the insurance cooling-off period is actually a distinct statutory requirement under financial regulations to prevent impulsive purchases, rather than a specific PDPA data protection mechanism, though it operates similarly by allowing withdrawal.  

    进入课程的第二部分，我们来看看同意的形式——即获取同意的实际方法。PDPA并没有严格规定任何强制性的形式，这意味着原则上，你可以随时以任何形式获取同意。然而，组织被期望进行合理的遵守，这意味着形式必须与情况相适应。同意可以是书面的，也可以是口头的。例如，如果一个人打电话给服务提供商要求通过电话开通服务，提供商可以问：“我们可以获得您的同意，使用您的个人数据来提供这些服务吗？” 如果打电话的人说是，那就是有效的口头同意。然而，PDPC明确表示，事后以书面形式跟进并确认该口头同意是一种良好的做法。教授问全班同学为什么这是一种好做法。多名学生回答说，这主要是一个证据问题。口头同意在事后是出了名的难以证明，除非整个通话被完美地记录并存档。拥有一份书面文件或声明表可以清楚地显示具体同意了哪些细节，如果客户后来声称他们没有同意特定条款，这就能解决争议。此外，书面记录能捕获给予同意的准确日期和时间。另一名学生提出了一个关于给银行或航空公司打电话的个人场景，自动语音会提示通话正在被录音。该学生想知道在这些通话中提供个人详细信息以验证身份是否构成口头同意。教授澄清说，如果你打电话给一家你已经开户的银行，银行并没有在寻求新的PDPA同意，因为当你最初签署全面的开户表格时，他们已经获得了所有必要的同意。另一名学生提到了保险代理人，他们打电话并获得口头同意，但随后提供七天的冷静期，客户可以在此期间撤回。教授指出，保险冷静期实际上是金融法规下的一项独特的法定要求，旨在防止冲动购买，而不是一个具体的PDPA数据保护机制，尽管它的运作方式类似，都允许撤回。  

  - [19:15:43 - 19:20:25] **Express vs. Implied Consent**

    Consent can also be categorized as either express or implied. Express consent means you use explicit words (written or spoken) to convey your agreement. Implied consent, on the other hand, does not use words; instead, consent is reasonably understood and inferred from an individual's conduct, behavior, or the surrounding circumstances. A good example of implied consent is the German European School Singapore case. The organization is a private international school, and the complainant was the parent of student "A B". The school conducted random drug testing and happened to select student A B. The school informed the parent that the child would be expelled if they refused to provide a hair sample for the test, or if the test came back positive. After some legal back-and-forth about whether this was an unreasonable condition, the PDPC ultimately ruled it was not unreasonable and focused on the implied consent aspect. The PDPC noted that while the school didn't actively seek fresh express consent on the day of the test, they had already obtained implied consent. This was because the school's bye-laws clearly included clause 5.8, which mandated random drug testing for students. Because the parents of student A B had access to these school rules, they were legally deemed to be notified. By voluntarily enrolling the child and keeping him in the school for ten years knowing these rules existed, the parents had implicitly consented to the drug testing and the associated collection of personal data. Therefore, the PDPC found no breach of the consent obligation. The professor then asked why organizations need to be extremely careful when relying on implied consent. Students correctly answered that organizations must ensure individuals actually have the means to access the information and understand what they are implicitly agreeing to. Furthermore, just like verbal consent, implied consent presents massive difficulties of proof—there is no signature or tick box to demonstrably prove the individual agreed. A student asked if warning signs at private estates stating "CCTV in operation" is an example of implied consent. The professor clarified that CCTV signs are technically not implied consent, but rather a form of notification. When you see a clear CCTV sign and choose to walk in anyway, you are allowing the recording, provided the sign was prominently displayed and not hidden.  

    同意也可以分为明示同意或默示同意（Implied Consent）。明示同意意味着你使用明确的词语（书面或口头）来传达你的同意。另一方面，默示同意不使用词语；相反，同意是从个人的行为、举止或周围环境中合理地被理解和推断出来的。关于默示同意的一个好例子是新加坡德国欧洲学校（German European School Singapore）案。该组织是一所私立国际学校，投诉人是学生“A B”的家长。学校进行了随机的药物测试，碰巧选中了学生 A B。学校通知家长，如果他们拒绝提供用于测试的头发样本，或者测试结果呈阳性，孩子将被开除。在经历了一些关于这是否属于不合理条件的法律交锋后，PDPC 最终裁定这并不不合理，并将重点放在了默示同意方面。PDPC 指出，虽然学校在测试当天没有主动寻求新的明示同意，但他们已经获得了默示同意。这是因为学校的章程明确包含了第 5.8 条款，该条款强制要求对学生进行随机药物测试。由于学生 A B 的父母可以获取这些学校规定，在法律上他们被视为已收到通知。在知道这些规定存在的情况下，父母自愿让孩子入学并在该校就读了十年，这意味着父母默示同意了药物测试以及相关的个人数据收集。因此，PDPC 认定没有违反同意义务。教授随后问，为什么组织在依赖默示同意时需要极其小心。学生们正确地回答说，组织必须确保个人实际上有途径获取信息，并了解他们默认同意的内容。此外，就像口头同意一样，默示同意在证据方面存在巨大的困难——没有签名或勾选框能明确证明个人同意了。有学生问，私人庄园里标明“闭路电视运作中”的警告标志是否是默示同意的一个例子。教授澄清说，闭路电视标志在技术上不是默示同意，而是一种通知形式。当你看到一个清晰的闭路电视标志并仍然选择走进去时，你就是在允许录像，前提是该标志被醒目地展示，而不是隐藏起来。  

  - [19:20:25 - 19:27:45] **Positive vs. Negative Consent**

    The final dimension of consent form is positive versus negative consent. Positive consent is obtained through an active, affirmative action taken by the individual, such as physically signing a form or explicitly ticking an empty box. Negative consent is the exact opposite: it is inferred because the individual failed to take a specific action. Examples include failing to untick a box that the organization has already pre-ticked for you, or staying silent when asked, "Do you have any objections?". The professor showed a slide comparing a good example (positive consent where a user ticks their preferred marketing channels) against a bad example (a pre-ticked box that assumes consent if left alone). The PDPC is fairly clear that organizations should avoid negative consent.  

    When asked why, a student correctly identified the fundamental problem with negative consent: it is inherently ambiguous. When an individual fails to untick a pre-ticked box, it might mean they genuinely read and agreed to it, but it could equally mean they simply missed it, the font was too small, or they were rushing and clicked past it by mistake. Because there is no affirmative action, it fails to prove that the individual expressly understood and agreed to the clause. The professor agreed, noting that while negative consent isn't completely impossible to use legally, it is highly risky. To get away with it, an organization would have to make the negative consent mechanism super large and painstakingly obvious to eliminate any claim of misunderstanding, which most organizations fail to do. A student argued that relying on negative consent might actually be legally invalid altogether. The professor validated this, stating there is a strong legal argument against it based on the fact that the PDPA already prescribes specific opt-out mechanisms elsewhere, making a generic pre-ticked opt-out legally sketchy. A student asked what happens if a complicated form is in a language (like Chinese) that the user doesn't fully understand, making them guess. The professor noted that while Singapore law generally has a bias assuming you read and understood what you signed, the language barrier makes negative consent even more dangerous, as it provides another highly plausible reason why a user simply failed to uncheck a box. Finally, the professor compared Singapore's stance to international laws. For example, the EU GDPR explicitly defines consent as a "clear affirmative action," and the Indian DPDPA contains very similar language. Both of these robust frameworks explicitly and permanently rule out negative consent because a failure to act (not checking a box) can never constitute a clear affirmative action. The professor then called for a ten-minute break.  

    同意形式的最后一个维度是积极同意（Positive Consent）与消极同意（Negative Consent）。积极同意是通过个人采取的主动、肯定的行动获得的，例如亲自在表格上签名或明确勾选一个空框。消极同意则完全相反：它是因为个人未能采取特定行动而推断出来的。例子包括未能取消勾选组织已经为你预先勾选的框，或者在被问及“您有什么异议吗？”时保持沉默。教授展示了一张幻灯片，对比了一个好例子（积极同意，用户主动勾选他们喜欢的营销渠道）和一个坏例子（预先勾选的框，如果不管它就假定同意）。PDPC 相当明确地指出，组织应该避免使用消极同意。  

    当被问及原因时，一名学生准确地指出了消极同意的根本问题：它天生就模棱两可。当个人未能取消预先勾选的框时，这可能意味着他们真的阅读并同意了它，但也同样可能意味着他们只是没看到它，字体太小了，或者他们赶时间，错误地点击跳过了。因为没有肯定的行动，它无法证明个人明确理解并同意该条款。教授表示赞同，指出虽然消极同意在法律上并非完全不能使用，但它的风险极高。为了侥幸成功，组织必须使消极同意机制变得超级大且极其明显，以消除任何关于误解的借口，而大多数组织都未能做到这一点。一名学生认为，依赖消极同意在法律上可能实际上完全无效。教授肯定了这一点，指出有一个强有力的法律论据反对它：PDPA 已经在其他地方规定了特定的选择退出机制，这使得通用的预先勾选选择退出在法律上站不住脚。有学生问，如果一份复杂的表格使用的是用户不完全理解的语言（如中文），导致他们只能靠猜，会发生什么情况。教授指出，虽然新加坡法律通常偏向于假设你阅读并理解了你所签署的内容，但语言障碍使消极同意变得更加危险，因为它提供了一个非常合理的理由，解释了为什么用户只是未能取消勾选一个框。最后，教授将新加坡的立场与国际法进行了比较。例如，欧盟 GDPR 明确将同意定义为“明确的肯定性行动”，而印度 DPDPA 也包含非常相似的措辞。这两个健全的框架都明确且永久地排除了消极同意，因为不采取行动（不勾选框）永远不可能构成明确的肯定性行动。随后，教授宣布休息十分钟。  

  - [19:42:31 - 19:52:31] **Case Study: EFG University and Unreasonable Tying**

    To illustrate these concepts, the professor introduced a realistic hypothetical case study involving EFG University. During the pandemic, EFG University conducted remote examinations via video conferencing for its international students. To prevent cheating, EFG utilized remote proctoring, requiring students to keep their webcams on while administrators manually watched the live video feeds. Students had already provided actual consent for this specific manual monitoring. However, EFG grew concerned that human administrators might miss instances of cheating when monitoring hundreds of students simultaneously. To solve this, EFG contracted with IHG, a software service provider, to automatically analyze the video recordings using an artificial intelligence system to detect cheating. Because they had no prior consent for AI analysis, EFG simply amended its privacy policy to state that video recordings would now be automatically analyzed by AI, and explicitly made consent to this new policy mandatory for all students taking exams. The professor posed the question: Did EFG successfully obtain actual consent? A student argued that simply changing the policy and requiring acceptance to continue using the service is problematic because nobody reads policy updates. The professor agreed, noting that EFG failed to actively bring this major change to the students' attention. Another student pointed out that this was a massive shift in scope—moving from human observation to third-party AI data processing—which requires a much higher level of explicit re-consenting. Furthermore, the professor highlighted the issue of making the consent mandatory. By telling students they cannot take their exams unless they agree to the AI processing, EFG risks committing "unreasonable tying." The class debated this: some students argued the AI was technically necessary to properly provide a secure examination service, while others argued it merely made the university's job easier and wasn't strictly necessary for the student to sit for the exam. The professor noted that the regulator's stance on this is debatable. A student then asked if a company could just include a clause saying, "You consent that we may update our policy from time to time." The professor explained that such blanket clauses are legally ineffective; if they worked, every company would use them to bypass consent entirely. For existing students, the proper method would have been to push a direct email requiring them to actively review and tick a new checkbox specifically for the AI processing, rather than quietly changing the rules.

    为了说明这些概念，教授介绍了一个涉及 EFG 大学的、非常贴近现实的假设案例研究。在疫情期间，EFG 大学通过视频会议为国际学生进行远程考试。为了防止作弊，EFG 采用了远程监考，要求学生保持网络摄像头开启，由管理员人工观看实时视频流。学生们已经为这种特定的人工监控提供了实际同意。然而，EFG 开始担心，当同时监控数百名学生时，人工管理员可能会漏掉作弊情况。为了解决这个问题，EFG 与软件服务提供商 IHG 签订了合同，使用人工智能系统自动分析视频记录以检测作弊。因为他们之前没有获得进行 AI 分析的同意，EFG 只是修改了其隐私政策，声明视频记录现在将由 AI 自动分析，并明确规定所有参加考试的学生必须同意这项新政策。教授提出了一个问题：EFG 是否成功获得了实际同意？一名学生认为，仅仅更改政策并要求接受才能继续使用服务是有问题的，因为没有人会去读政策更新。教授表示赞同，指出 EFG 未能主动提请学生注意这一重大变化。另一名学生指出，这是范围上的巨大转变——从人类观察转向第三方 AI 数据处理——这需要更高水平的明确重新同意。此外，教授强调了强制要求同意的问题。通过告诉学生除非同意 AI 处理否则不能参加考试，EFG 面临着“不合理捆绑”的风险。全班对此进行了辩论：一些学生认为，在技术上，AI 对于妥善提供安全的考试服务是必要的，而另一些学生则认为它只是让大学的工作变得更容易，对于学生参加考试而言并非绝对必要。教授指出，监管机构对此的立场是存在争议的。随后有学生问，公司是否可以只包含一个条款，写明“您同意我们可能不时更新我们的政策”。教授解释说，这种一揽子条款在法律上是无效的；如果它们起作用，每家公司都会使用它们来完全绕过同意。对于在校生，正确的方法应该是发送一封直接电子邮件，要求他们主动审阅并专门为 AI 处理勾选一个新的复选框，而不是悄悄地改变规则。

  - [19:52:31 - 20:06:00] **The Three Types of Deemed Consent**

    Moving past actual consent, the professor introduced the second primary method of fulfilling the obligation: deemed consent. Deemed consent occurs when an organization is legally allowed to assume the individual has given consent without explicitly asking for it. There are three main types. The first is "deemed consent from a third party." This applies when an individual gives consent to Organization A to disclose their data to Organization B. Logically, the individual is therefore deemed to have consented to Organization B collecting that data. However, Organization B must exercise due diligence. Before relying on this, they must verify that Organization A actually obtained the initial consent. For example, if Sarah gives her friend Jane's details to a spa for a referral program, the spa consultant must ask Sarah to confirm that Jane knew about and consented to this disclosure before the spa can legally record the data. The second type is "deemed consent by conduct." This happens when an individual voluntarily provides their personal data for an objectively obvious purpose. For instance, if Sarah hands her credit card to a cashier to pay for a $49.99 treatment, she is deemed by her conduct to have consented to the processing of her payment details. Similarly, if Eva proceeds with a medical checkup, she implicitly consents to the clinic collecting her blood pressure and weight. If Tina calls a taxi hotline and gives her name and number, she is deemed to consent to the taxi company using that data to send a cab. However, the taxi company cannot stretch this deemed consent to suddenly send her marketing materials for a limousine service, as that purpose was not objectively obvious. The professor highlighted a PDPC case involving Actxa, a smart scale company. A user voluntarily provided registration data (height, weight, date of birth) to use the companion app, which the PDPC ruled fell under deemed consent by conduct because the purpose was obvious. However, the app also quietly transmitted raw body data from the scale to Actxa's servers. The PDPC ruled this was a breach because the purpose of collecting that specific raw data was not sufficiently clear to the user. The third type is "deemed consent by contractual necessity." When you provide data to an organization to fulfill a contract, you are deemed to consent to that organization sharing your data with downstream third parties (like banks or payment processors) strictly as long as it is reasonably necessary to complete that specific transaction. A student asked if this covers "data intermediaries," and the professor clarified that it covers anyone in the processing chain whose involvement is fundamentally necessary for the contract to be fulfilled.

    讲完实际同意后，教授介绍了履行义务的第二种主要方法：视同同意。视同同意是指在法律允许的情况下，组织可以在没有明确要求的情况下，假设个人已经给予了同意。主要有三种类型。第一种是“第三方的视同同意”。这适用于个人同意组织 A 将其数据披露给组织 B 的情况。从逻辑上讲，因此个人被视为已同意组织 B 收集该数据。然而，组织 B 必须尽到尽职调查的责任。在依赖此规定之前，他们必须核实组织 A 确实获得了最初的同意。例如，如果莎拉为了推荐计划将她朋友简的详细信息提供给水疗中心，水疗中心顾问必须要求莎拉确认简知道并同意了这项披露，然后水疗中心才能合法记录这些数据。第二种类型是“通过行为视同同意”。当个人为了一个客观上显而易见的目的自愿提供其个人数据时，就会发生这种情况。例如，如果莎拉将信用卡递给收银员支付 49.99 美元的护理费，她的行为即被视为同意处理其付款详细信息。同样，如果伊娃继续进行体检，她就隐性同意了诊所收集她的血压和体重。如果蒂娜拨打出租车热线并留下她的姓名和号码，她就被视为同意出租车公司使用该数据来派车。然而，出租车公司不能无限扩大这种视同同意的范围，突然向她发送豪华轿车服务的营销材料，因为这个目的在客观上并不明显。教授重点介绍了 PDPC 关于智能体脂秤公司 Actxa 的一个案例。一名用户自愿提供注册数据（身高、体重、出生日期）以使用配套的应用程序，PDPC 裁定这属于通过行为视同同意，因为目的显而易见。然而，该应用程序还悄悄地将秤上的原始身体数据传输到 Actxa 的服务器。PDPC 裁定这是一种违规行为，因为收集这些特定原始数据的目的对用户来说不够明确。第三种类型是“因合同必要性视同同意”。当您向某个组织提供数据以履行合同时，只要为了完成该特定交易具有合理必要性，您就被视为同意该组织将您的数据与下游第三方（如银行或支付处理商）共享。一名学生问这是否涵盖“数据中介”，教授澄清说，它涵盖处理链中其参与对于履行合同具有根本必要性的任何人。

  - [20:06:00 - 20:29:28] **Deemed Consent by Notification & Implicit Actions**

    The final and most complex form of deemed consent is "deemed consent by notification." This mechanism allows an organization to notify an individual about their intention to collect, use, or disclose data, and if the individual fails to opt out, consent is legally deemed. Because this relies on a negative action (which is inherently risky), the law imposes three strict mandatory safeguards. First, it is strictly forbidden to use this method for direct marketing purposes. Second, the organization must conduct an internal "adverse effect assessment." They must evaluate if the data processing poses any harm to the individual, implement mitigating measures, and prove there are no likely residual adverse effects before proceeding. Third, the organization must provide appropriate notification through direct channels (like email or SMS, rather than an obscure website notice) stating the intention, purpose, and a reasonable opt-out period. For example, a health app that wants to use existing step-counter data to build a new personalized weight loss program could use the app's interface to notify users, giving them 14 days to opt out. Conversely, an association deploying facial recognition sensors at an exhibition cannot rely on deemed consent by notification, because the moment a visitor walks into the hall, data collection begins, offering no reasonable prior opt-out period.

    A student asked for an example of a scenario with a clear adverse effect. The professor cited an adversarial relationship, such as a bank using data to investigate a customer for potential fraud. If the bank finds fraud, they will freeze the account—a highly adverse impact. In such cases, the bank cannot rely on deemed consent by notification and must instead rely on statutory exceptions. Another student asked if placing a "CCTV in Operation" sign or an event photography notice relies on deemed consent by notification. The professor clarified a nuanced legal distinction: walking past a highly visible warning sign and entering a premises is actually considered a form of *actual* consent (specifically, actual consent implied by physical conduct), rather than the statutory mechanism of "deemed consent by notification," which is a specific legal fallback designed for situations where explicit affirmative consent isn't captured but an opt-out window was provided. Following this complex breakdown, the professor initiated a five-minute break.

    最后一种也是最复杂的一种视同同意形式是“通过通知视同同意”。这种机制允许组织通知个人其收集、使用或披露数据的意图，如果个人没有选择退出，则在法律上视同同意。因为这依赖于一种消极行动（这本质上是有风险的），法律施加了三项严格的强制性保障措施。首先，严禁将此方法用于直接营销目的。其次，组织必须进行内部的“不良影响评估”。他们必须评估数据处理是否会对个人造成任何伤害，实施缓解措施，并证明在继续操作之前不存在可能的残余不良影响。第三，组织必须通过直接渠道（如电子邮件或短信，而不是不起眼的网站通知）提供适当的通知，说明意图、目的以及合理的选择退出期限。例如，一个健康应用程序想要使用现有的计步器数据来构建一个新的个性化减肥计划，它可以使用应用程序的界面来通知用户，给他们 14 天的时间来选择退出。相反，在展览会上部署面部识别传感器的协会不能依赖通过通知视同同意，因为访客步入大厅的那一刻起，数据收集就开始了，根本没有提供合理的提前选择退出期限。

    有学生要求举一个有明显不良影响场景的例子。教授引用了对抗性关系，例如银行使用数据调查客户的潜在欺诈行为。如果银行发现欺诈行为，他们将冻结账户——这是一种高度不良的影响。在这种情况下，银行不能依赖通过通知视同同意，而必须依赖法定例外情况。另一名学生问，放置“闭路电视运作中”的标志或活动摄影通知是否属于依赖通过通知视同同意。教授澄清了一个微妙的法律区别：走过高度可见的警告标志并进入场所，实际上被认为是*实际*同意的一种形式（具体来说，是通过身体行为暗示的实际同意），而不是“通过通知视同同意”的法定机制，后者是一种特定的法律后备方案，专为没有捕获明确肯定同意但提供了选择退出窗口的情况而设计。在这段复杂的详细分析之后，教授宣布休息五分钟。

  - [20:29:28 - 20:49:00] **The Right to Withdraw Consent and its Consequences**

    Returning from the break, the professor addressed the mechanics of withdrawing consent. Organizations are legally required to allow individuals to withdraw their consent (whether actual or deemed) at any time. To facilitate this, companies must maintain a clear, easily accessible withdrawal policy that explains how to submit a notice of withdrawal, whom to contact, and distinguishes between data required for core services versus optional data. The withdrawal process is triggered when the individual gives notice. While the law is vague on exact timelines, the standard rule of thumb allows the organization 10 business days to effectuate the withdrawal. Importantly, the very first step upon receiving a withdrawal notice is that the organization must inform the individual of the likely legal or service consequences of pulling their data. The organization must then cease collecting, using, and disclosing the data, and crucially, force their internal data intermediaries and agents to stop as well. However, the professor corrected a common misconception: withdrawing consent does not force the organization to immediately permanently delete the data. An individual cannot demand instant deletion under this clause, as the organization is allowed to retain the data for legitimate business or legal purposes, such as maintaining audit logs or managing ongoing warranty periods. A student presented a scenario regarding Andy, who withdraws marketing consent from Y Electronics. The professor explained that Y Electronics must stop sending ads, must tell its hired marketing agents to stop, but does not need to hunt down independent third parties who previously received the data to inform them; Andy would have to contact those third parties separately. The discussion then shifted to the prohibition of restricting withdrawals. An organization cannot write a contract explicitly forbidding an individual from withdrawing consent, nor can they charge a direct administrative fee just for processing the withdrawal request, as this illegally deters the action. However, withdrawal can carry valid downstream legal consequences. For instance, if an individual withdraws consent for necessary billing data from a telecom provider, the telco can no longer provide the service, resulting in an early termination of the contract. The telco is absolutely allowed to charge standard early termination fees in this scenario. Students raised complex scenarios, such as credit card companies offering a discount in exchange for marketing consent. If the user later withdraws consent, can the company charge a fee to claw back the discount? The professor noted this is a grey area; the regulator would scrutinize whether the charge genuinely reflects the loss of an upfront discount or if it acts as a punitive deterrent masquerading as a fee. If the withdrawal simply results in a natural degradation of service quality because the data is missing, that is perfectly legal; but artificially degrading a service purely to punish the user for protecting their privacy is prohibited.

    休息回来后，教授探讨了撤回同意的机制。法律要求组织必须允许个人随时撤回其同意（无论是实际同意还是视同同意）。为了便于操作，公司必须维持一项清晰、易于访问的撤回政策，解释如何提交撤回通知、联系谁，并区分核心服务所需的数据与可选数据。撤回程序在个人发出通知时触发。虽然法律在确切的时间表上比较模糊，但标准的经验法则是允许组织在 10 个工作日内使撤回生效。重要的是，收到撤回通知后的第一步是，组织必须告知个人撤回其数据可能带来的法律或服务后果。然后，组织必须停止收集、使用和披露该数据，关键是还要迫使其内部数据中介和代理也停止。然而，教授纠正了一个常见的误解：撤回同意并不迫使组织立即永久删除数据。个人不能根据本条款要求立即删除，因为组织被允许出于合法的商业或法律目的（例如保留审计日志或管理正在进行的保修期）保留数据。一名学生提出了一个关于安迪的场景，他撤回了对 Y 电子公司的营销同意。教授解释说，Y 电子公司必须停止发送广告，必须告诉其雇佣的营销代理停止，但不需要去追踪以前收到数据的独立第三方来通知他们；安迪必须单独联系这些第三方。随后的讨论转向了禁止限制撤回的问题。组织不能起草明确禁止个人撤回同意的合同，也不能仅仅为了处理撤回请求而收取直接的行政费用，因为这非法阻碍了该行动。然而，撤回可能会带来有效的下游法律后果。例如，如果个人向电信提供商撤回了必要计费数据的同意，电信公司将无法再提供服务，从而导致合同提前终止。在这种情况下，电信公司绝对被允许收取标准的提前终止费。学生们提出了复杂的场景，例如信用卡公司提供折扣以换取营销同意。如果用户后来撤回同意，公司可以收取费用来收回折扣吗？教授指出这是一个灰色地带；监管机构将仔细审查该费用是否真实反映了前期折扣的损失，或者它是否是伪装成费用的惩罚性威慑。如果撤回只是因为缺少数据而导致服务质量自然下降，那是完全合法的；但纯粹为了惩罚用户保护隐私而人为降低服务质量是被禁止的。

  - [20:49:00 - 21:14:16] **LinkedIn Case Study and Public Policy Exceptions**

    To conclude the lecture, the professor analyzed a highly relevant real-world scenario involving LinkedIn. According to a recent article, LinkedIn quietly updated its privacy policy to allow the platform to scrape user content and personal data to train generative AI models (including third-party models like OpenAI) to power a new writing assistant. Users were outraged because they received no direct notification. LinkedIn argued that users could opt out by navigating deep into their privacy settings and toggling off "Use my data for training content creation AI models," though the policy stated that opting out would not reverse any training that had already occurred. The professor asked the class to evaluate this against the PDPA consent frameworks. The class quickly identified multiple severe breaches. First, LinkedIn relied exclusively on negative consent (an opt-out model), which is legally risky. Worse, the opt-out was essentially ineffective since past training couldn't be undone. Second, the notification was incredibly indirect—burying a change in a massive policy update document rather than pushing a direct pop-up to users. A student pointed out the issue of inactive users, who would fail to uncheck the box simply because they hadn't logged in, highlighting exactly why negative consent is heavily frowned upon. Another major issue raised was the purpose of the data collection. A student argued that training a third-party generative AI model is a radically unexpected departure from the original purpose users consented to (professional networking). Even if a clever corporate lawyer tried to stretch an old, vague clause to cover AI training, sharing that data with external entities like OpenAI would violate reasonable user expectations. To do this legally, the professor explained, LinkedIn should have either used a mandatory pop-up requiring active actual consent (which is administratively heavy) or properly executed deemed consent by notification. The latter would require a formal adverse effect assessment, sending a direct email to all users explicitly outlining the AI training, and instituting a strict waiting period (e.g., 14 days) during which no training occurs, allowing users a fair window to opt out. Finally, a student asked how this corporate data protection law reconciles with government policies like the Human Organ Transplant Act (HOTA), which operates on a strict, automatic opt-out basis. The professor clarified that statutory public policy initiatives governed by the state operate under entirely different philosophical and legal considerations than corporate data privacy. The government leverages a heavy-handed, default-in framework to maximize public benefit, which supersedes the PDPA's commercial frameworks.

    为了总结这堂课，教授分析了一个与 LinkedIn 相关的高度相关的现实场景。根据最近的一篇文章，LinkedIn 悄悄更新了其隐私政策，允许该平台抓取用户内容和个人数据，以训练生成式 AI 模型（包括像 OpenAI 这样的第三方模型），从而为一个新的写作助手提供支持。用户对此感到愤怒，因为他们没有收到任何直接通知。LinkedIn 辩称，用户可以通过深入其隐私设置并关闭“使用我的数据来训练内容创建 AI 模型”来选择退出，尽管政策声明选择退出不会撤销已经发生的任何训练。教授要求全班根据 PDPA 同意框架对此进行评估。全班很快找出了多项严重的违规行为。首先，LinkedIn 完全依赖于消极同意（一种选择退出模式），这在法律上是有风险的。更糟糕的是，这种选择退出本质上是无效的，因为过去的训练无法撤销。其次，通知非常间接——将更改埋藏在庞大的政策更新文档中，而不是向用户推送直接的弹出窗口。一名学生指出了不活跃用户的问题，他们只是因为没有登录而未能取消勾选框，这恰恰突显了为什么消极同意极其不受欢迎。提出的另一个主要问题是数据收集的目的。一名学生认为，训练第三方生成式 AI 模型完全偏离了用户最初同意的目的（职业社交），出乎意料。即使一位聪明的公司律师试图无限扩大一个模糊的旧条款来涵盖 AI 训练，与 OpenAI 等外部实体共享这些数据也会违背用户的合理期望。教授解释说，为了合法地做到这一点，LinkedIn 应该要么使用强制弹窗要求主动的实际同意（这在管理上很繁重），要么妥善执行通过通知视同同意。后者将需要进行正式的不良影响评估，向所有用户发送直接电子邮件，明确概述 AI 训练，并制定严格的等待期（例如 14 天），在此期间不进行任何训练，从而为用户提供一个公平的选择退出窗口。最后，一名学生问，这种企业数据保护法如何与政府政策（如《人体器官移植法案》(HOTA)）相协调，后者是在严格的自动选择退出基础上运作的。教授澄清说，由国家主导的法定公共政策举措在运作时的哲学和法律考量与企业数据隐私完全不同。政府利用强硬的默认加入框架来最大化公共利益，这凌驾于 PDPA 的商业框架之上。
***

# Seminar 4 (gemini)
## Week 4 Readings
- AGKCP at 12.55-13.4.
## Week 4 Summary
- **Exceptions to the Consent Obligation 同意义务的例外情况**
  - **Legitimate Interest Exception:** Allows data processing without consent if it pursues a tangible, non-speculative benefit. The "workflow" requires identifying the interest, conducting a strict **adverse effect assessment**, mitigating identified harms, and ensuring the legitimate interest outweighs any residual negative impact. Organizations must publicly disclose their reliance on this exception.
  - **Business Improvement Exception:** Singapore-specific framework designed to facilitate business innovation (e.g., developing new goods, operational processes, or personalized recommendations). Requires that the purpose cannot reasonably be achieved without identifiable data, and the usage must be **reasonable**. It offers a more straightforward compliance path than Legitimate Interest as it skips the complex adverse effect assessment.
  - **Research & Public Availability Exceptions:** Research exception is heavily guarded, requiring a **clear public benefit** (rarely met by private companies) and anonymized publication. Public Availability applies to data generally accessible with few restrictions. It includes "publicly observable" data, provided it is collected via **reasonably expected means** in locations **open to the public**.
  - **合法利益例外：** 如果追求有形且非推测性的利益，则允许在未经同意的情况下处理数据。其“工作流程”要求确定利益，进行严格的**不利影响评估**，减轻已发现的损害，并确保合法利益超过任何残余的负面影响。组织必须公开披露其对此例外的依赖。
  - **业务改进例外：** 新加坡特有的框架，旨在促进业务创新（例如，开发新商品、运营流程或个性化推荐）。要求如果不使用可识别数据就无法合理实现该目的，并且使用必须是**合理的**。它跳过了复杂的不利影响评估，提供了一条比合法利益更直接的合规路径。
  - **研究与公开可用例外：** 研究例外受到严格限制，需要有**明确的公共利益**（私营公司极少能满足）和匿名化发布。公开可用性适用于限制很少且公众普遍可访问的数据。它包括“可公开观察”的数据，前提是在**向公众开放**的地点通过**合理预期的方式**收集。
- **The Purpose Limitation Obligation 目的限制义务**
  - **The Safety Net Concept:** An independent obligation that operates entirely separate from consent. Even with valid consent or a valid exception, organizations can only process data for purposes a **reasonable person** would consider appropriate. It prevents blanket, preemptive consent from being exploited.
  - **Evaluating Reasonableness - Nature & Data Kind:** Assess the nature of the purpose (illegal, harmful, or extortionate purposes are strictly prohibited). Next, evaluate the kind of data; **sensitive personal data** (like financial indebtedness) drastically raises the threshold for what constitutes a reasonable purpose.
  - **Evaluating Reasonableness - Manner of Processing:** Focuses on the "algorithm" of proportionality. Is the processing method actually **effective**? More importantly, is it **invasive**? Organizations must ask if a less intrusive method exists. In AI and big data, the massive loss of privacy must not be disproportionate to the technological or economic benefit.
  - **安全网概念：** 一项完全独立于同意运作的独立义务。即使拥有有效的同意或有效的例外，组织也只能出于**理性人**认为合适的目地处理数据。它防止了全面、预防性的同意被滥用。
  - **评估合理性 - 性质与数据种类：** 评估目的的性质（严禁非法、有害或敲诈勒索的目的）。接下来，评估数据种类；**敏感个人数据**（如财务负债）会大幅提高合理目的的门槛。
  - **评估合理性 - 处理方式：** 侧重于相称性的“算法”。处理方法实际上**有效**吗？更重要的是，它是否具有**侵入性**？组织必须思考是否存在侵入性较小的方法。在人工智能和大数据领域，大规模的隐私丧失绝不能与技术或经济利益不成比例。
## Week 4 Learning Tips
Welcome to this week's review! As you dive into these frameworks, I want you to step back and look at the big picture. A common pitfall for students studying data privacy is treating governance merely as a compliance checklist—checking off boxes to avoid fines. Instead, I encourage you to build an intuition for the "why" behind the frameworks. Think of these laws as a dynamic balancing algorithm between business innovation and fundamental human dignity. When you study the Legitimate Interest exception, don't just memorize the three steps; visualize the invisible scale where an organization's commercial goals are constantly weighed against a user's potential distress.

欢迎来到本周的复习！在你们深入研究这些框架时，我希望你们能退一步，着眼于全局。学生在学习数据隐私时常犯的一个错误是，仅仅将治理视为一份合规清单——只是为了避免罚款而逐项打勾。相反，我鼓励你们培养对这些框架背后“为什么”的直觉。把这些法律想象成商业创新与基本人类尊严之间动态平衡的算法。当你们学习合法利益例外时，不要只是死记硬背那三个步骤；要在脑海中想象一个无形的天平，组织的一端是商业目标，另一端则是用户可能遭受的痛苦，这两者在不断地进行权衡。

One of the most crucial conceptual hurdles this week is mastering the Purpose Limitation Obligation. You must fundamentally separate "Consent" from "Purpose Limitation" in your mind. It is easy to mistakenly assume that if a user clicks "I Agree," the company has a blank check to process their data. Remember the "safety net" principle: Purpose Limitation exists precisely because users often make uninformed or foolish choices. When analyzing case studies like the "Detective Mode" laptop tracking, challenge yourself to evaluate the proportionality. Is the method effective? Yes. But is the immense invasion of privacy strictly necessary? Developing this critical lens—always asking if a less invasive alternative exists—is what separates a rule-follower from a true privacy engineering expert.

本周最关键的概念障碍之一是掌握目的限制义务。你们必须在头脑中从根本上将“同意”与“目的限制”区分开来。人们很容易错误地认为，只要用户点击了“我同意”，公司就拥有了处理他们数据的空白支票。请记住“安全网”原则：目的限制之所以存在，恰恰是因为用户经常做出不知情或愚蠢的选择。在分析诸如“侦探模式”笔记本电脑跟踪等案例研究时，要挑战自己去评估其相称性。这种方法有效吗？是的。但是，这种对隐私的巨大侵犯是绝对必要的吗？培养这种批判性的视角——始终追问是否存在侵入性更小的替代方案——正是区分规则遵守者和真正的隐私工程专家的关键所在。
## Week 4 Lecture
  - [18:35:37 - 18:37:15] **Introduction to Consent Exceptions**

    Welcome back. In the previous seminar, we discussed the consent obligation, covering both actual consent and implied consent. For today's lesson, we are going to begin by looking at the final way by which an organisation can comply with the consent obligation, which is to rely upon one of the exceptions to consent. If you look inside the legislation, you will see that there is actually a very long list of exceptions. Some of these are pretty straightforward—for example, in emergency situations, you can do away with the consent requirement. For our purposes in this course, we are going to look at just four of the more significant exceptions: the legitimate interest exception, the business improvement exception, the research exception, and the public availability exception. Because in practice, these are the four exceptions that organisations tend to rely on much more frequently than the others. Let's begin by looking at the legitimate interest exception.  

    欢迎回来。在之前的研讨课中，我们讨论了同意义务，包括实际同意和默示同意。在今天的课程中，我们将首先探讨组织遵守同意义务的最后一种方式，那就是依赖同意的例外情况。如果你查阅立法，你会发现实际上有很长一串的例外情况。其中一些非常直接——例如，在紧急情况下，你可以免除同意的要求。为了我们这门课程的学习目的，我们将只关注四个更重要的例外情况：合法利益例外、业务改进例外、研究例外和公开可用例外。因为在实践中，组织依赖这四个例外的频率远高于其他例外。让我们先从合法利益例外看起。

  - [18:37:15 - 18:41:10] **Legitimate Interest Exception: Identifying the Interest**

    The basic idea behind this exception is that if the organisation is trying to pursue some legitimate interest, then it may collect, use, or disclose personal data without actually obtaining consent, provided doing so is in the legitimate interests of the organisation or another person. However, it is not so straightforward. You can't just say, "Oh, I have a legitimate purpose, therefore I don't have to get consent." To rely on this exception, there are specific requirements that organisations must fulfil. First, you need to identify and articulate what exactly your legitimate interest is. Number two, similar to deemed consent by notification which we mentioned in the previous seminar, you also need to conduct an adverse effect assessment to see if what you are doing will likely have a negative impact on the individual. There is a slight difference here which I will highlight in a bit. The last thing you need to do is to disclose that you are relying on the exception; you can't do it secretly. You must tell people you are using their personal data for this particular legitimate interest. Let's look at the first requirement: articulating your legitimate interest. What is the important reason you have for processing the data? When articulating this, you should identify the specific benefits arising from the collection, use, or disclosure—such as ensuring safety or preventing fraud. You should also identify the beneficiaries; while it will often be the organisation itself, it could also be other people or the general public. Furthermore, you must ensure that whatever benefit you are chasing is a real and present benefit, not something that is merely speculative. It cannot be something which *might* possibly happen; you need to demonstrate that this benefit will tangibly arise if you process the data. One important limitation to note is that you cannot rely on the legitimate interest exception to send direct marketing messages, which is a restriction similar to deemed consent by notification.  

    这个例外背后的基本理念是，如果组织试图追求某种合法利益，或者为了组织或他人的合法利益，那么它可以在未实际获得同意的情况下收集、使用或披露个人数据。然而，事情并没有那么简单。你不能只是说，“哦，我有一个合法目的，所以我不需要获得同意。”要依赖这个例外，组织必须满足一些具体要求。首先，你需要识别并清楚阐明你的合法利益究竟是什么。第二，与我们在上一次研讨课中提到的通过通知默示同意类似，你还需要进行不利影响评估，看看你正在做的事情是否可能对个人产生负面影响。这里有一点细微的区别，我稍后会强调。你需要做的最后一件事是披露你正在依赖该例外；你不能秘密进行。你必须告诉人们你正在将他们的个人数据用于这个特定的合法利益。让我们看看第一个要求：阐明你的合法利益。你处理数据的重要原因是什么？在阐明这一点时，你应该指出收集、使用或披露所带来的具体好处——比如确保安全或防止欺诈。你还应该确定受益人；虽然通常是组织本身，但也可能是其他人或公众。此外，你必须确保你追求的任何好处都是真实且现实存在的，而不仅仅是推测性的。它不能是某种可能发生的事情；你需要证明如果你处理数据，这种好处就会切实产生。需要注意的一个重要限制是，你不能依赖合法利益例外来发送直接营销信息，这与通过通知默示同意的限制相似。

  - [18:41:10 - 18:45:19] **Legitimate Interest Exception: Adverse Effect Assessment**

    The second step is to conduct the adverse effect assessment, identifying whether your actions will cause an adverse effect on the individual. If you look at the guidelines, they provide some interesting nuance as to what counts as an adverse effect. Physical harm, harassment, serious alarm, or distress all clearly count as adverse effects. You don't have to think too deeply about those. However, if you are looking at other potential forms of adverse effects, such as economic impacts, you will need to consider prevailing social norms. This is important because, in some circumstances, a decision based on personal data might look harmful to the individual, but it is generally regarded as acceptable by society. For example, if a bank processes your data to determine your creditworthiness and refuses to grant you a loan because of a poor credit rating, you could argue that is an adverse effect because you didn't get the money. Yet, based on prevailing social norms, this is not legally regarded as an adverse effect; it is an acceptable business decision. So, to predict the likely adverse effect on the individual, the organisation should consider all the circumstances. First, consider the severity and likelihood of the impact on the individual. Second, consider the nature and type of personal data being processed; naturally, sensitive data is more likely to cause harm. You should also assess whether the individual belongs to a vulnerable group, such as a child, who might be subject to discrimination. Furthermore, evaluate the extent of the collection, use, or disclosure and how the data will be processed. Consider the reasonableness of your purpose, and finally, determine whether any predictions or decisions arising from the processing are likely to cause physical harm, harassment, serious alarm, or distress to the individual.  

    第二步是进行不利影响评估，确定你的行为是否会对个人造成不利影响。如果你查阅指南，它们对什么算作不利影响提供了一些有趣的细微差别。身体伤害、骚扰、严重恐慌或痛苦，这些都明确算作不利影响。对于这些你不需要想得太深。然而，如果你在考虑其他潜在形式的不利影响，比如经济影响，你需要考虑普遍的社会规范。这很重要，因为在某些情况下，基于个人数据做出的决定可能看起来对个人有害，但这在社会上通常被认为是可接受的。例如，如果银行处理你的数据以确定你的信用度，并因为不良信用评级拒绝给你贷款，你可以争辩说这是一个不利影响，因为你没有得到这笔钱。然而，基于普遍的社会规范，这在法律上不被视为不利影响；这是一个可以接受的商业决定。因此，为了预测可能对个人产生的不利影响，组织应该考虑所有的具体情况。首先，考虑对个人影响的严重程度和可能性。其次，考虑正在处理的个人数据的性质和类型；自然，敏感数据更有可能造成伤害。你还应该评估该个人是否属于弱势群体，例如可能受到歧视的儿童。此外，评估收集、使用或披露的程度以及数据将如何处理。考虑你目的的合理性，最后确定由处理产生的任何预测或决定是否可能对个人造成身体伤害、骚扰、严重恐慌或痛苦。

  - [18:45:19 - 18:48:15] **Legitimate Interest Exception: Mitigation and Disclosure**

    After determining the likely adverse effects, there are two crucial things the organisation must do. First, the organisation must implement reasonable measures to eliminate, reduce the likelihood of, or mitigate any adverse effects. This is similar to the mitigation process in deemed consent by notification. However, what makes this exception different is that you do not have to eliminate the adverse effects completely. Under deemed consent by notification, you must get rid of the adverse effect entirely. For the legitimate interest exception, the law allows a balancing act. After implementing measures, the organisation must ensure that any residual adverse effects do not outweigh the legitimate interest. If the legitimate interest is more important and outweighs the adverse effects, you can carry on. If the legitimate interest does not outweigh the harm, then you cannot use this exception. Once you have completed this balancing assessment, organisations must retain the adverse effect assessment throughout the entire period of the collection, use, or disclosure of the data. The third point is disclosure. If you rely on this exception, you must provide the individual with reasonable access to information about the organisation's collection, use, or disclosure. In many cases, this is simply done by putting a notice in your data protection policy. In addition to this, the organisation must provide the business contact information of a person who is able to address individuals' queries about the reliance on the exception, which will usually be your Data Protection Officer (DPO). One important thing to bear in mind is that while you need to inform people you are relying on the exception, organisations need not disclose the adverse effect assessment itself to the public. You can keep that document secret until the PDPC asks for it.  

    在确定了可能的不利影响之后，组织必须做两件关键的事情。首先，组织必须采取合理措施，以消除、降低可能性或减轻任何不利影响。这与通过通知默示同意中的缓解过程类似。然而，这个例外的不同之处在于，你不需要完全消除不利影响。在通过通知默示同意下，你必须完全消除不利影响。对于合法利益例外，法律允许进行权衡。在实施措施后，组织必须确保任何残余的不利影响不会超过合法利益。如果合法利益更重要并超过了不利影响，你就可以继续。如果合法利益没有超过伤害，那么你就不能使用这个例外。一旦你完成了这种平衡评估，组织必须在收集、使用或披露数据的整个期间保留不利影响评估。第三点是披露。如果你依赖这个例外，你必须为个人提供合理途径，以获取有关组织收集、使用或披露的信息。在许多情况下，这只需通过在你的数据保护政策中发布通知即可完成。除此之外，组织还必须提供一个人的业务联系信息，该人能够解答个人关于依赖该例外的疑问，这通常是你的数据保护官 (DPO)。需要记住的一件重要事情是，虽然你需要通知人们你正在依赖该例外，但组织不需要向公众披露不利影响评估本身。你可以对该文件保密，直到 PDPC 要求查看。

  - [18:48:15 - 18:51:05] **Examples of Legitimate Interest: Fraud Detection and Hotel Skippers**

    Let me show you some examples of how this exception can apply in practice. The first example involves an insurance company that intends to collect, use, and disclose personal data about its customers' past insurance claims for fraud detection and prevention. This company suspects that some customers are making fraudulent claims. Here, the insurance company would conduct an assessment of legitimate interests. They would evaluate the adverse effects to see if they outweigh the legitimate interest. If the company assesses that the benefits of the collection, use, and disclosure outweigh any adverse effect, and they state in their data protection policy on their website that they are relying on this exception, then they can carry on processing the data without obtaining consent. Another interesting example involves preventing non-payment. Imagine several hotels intend to compile and share a blacklist of "hotel skippers"—meaning hotel guests with a track record of not fulfilling their payments for use of hotel services. This database would contain personal data of these skippers, such as full names, passport or NRIC numbers, amounts owed, and details of non-payment for guests who have two or more occurrences of non-payment. Again, these hotels conduct a joint assessment of legitimate interests, conclude that the benefits of detecting and preventing misuse of services outweigh any adverse effect to the individuals, and include this reliance in their respective data protection policies. In these two examples—fraud detection and prevention of non-payment—it is highly unlikely that the individuals (the fraudulent claimants or the hotel skippers) would ever grant you consent to process their data, because doing so would automatically lead to negative consequences for them. The legitimate interest exception is practically required here.  

    让我向你们展示一些这个例外在实践中如何应用的例子。第一个例子涉及一家保险公司，该公司打算收集、使用和披露有关其客户过去保险索赔的个人数据，用于欺诈检测和预防。该公司怀疑一些客户正在进行欺诈性索赔。在这里，保险公司将进行合法利益评估。他们会评估不利影响，看看这些影响是否超过了合法利益。如果公司评估收集、使用和披露带来的好处超过了任何不利影响，并在其网站的数据保护政策中声明他们依赖这个例外，那么他们就可以在不获取同意的情况下继续处理数据。另一个有趣的例子涉及防止未付款。想象一下，几家酒店打算编制并分享一份“逃单住客”的黑名单——这意味着那些有不支付酒店服务费记录的客人。该数据库将包含这些逃单者的个人数据，比如全名、护照或身份证号码、欠款金额，以及有两次或多次未付款记录的客人的未付款详情。同样，这些酒店进行了联合合法利益评估，得出结论认为发现和防止服务滥用的好处超过了对个人的任何不利影响，并在各自的数据保护政策中列入了对该例外的依赖。在这两个例子中——欺诈检测和防止未付款——个人（欺诈性索赔人或酒店逃单者）极不可能同意你处理他们的数据，因为这样做会自动给他们带来负面后果。在这里，合法利益例外几乎是必需的。

  - [18:51:05 - 18:53:27] **Student Q&A: Jurisdictional Differences and the Balancing Exercise**

    A student asked a great question regarding the hotel skippers example, wondering if this practice would be judged differently in the EU under the GDPR, or if it would be acceptable there as well. I explained that in the EU, there is also this concept of legitimate interests, but it is framed slightly differently. In Singapore, legitimate interest is merely an exception to the consent obligation; the default rule is that organisations should get consent unless there is a valid reason why they cannot. In other jurisdictions, like the EU GDPR, legitimate interest is a separate legal basis on par with consent. Under Article 6 of the EU GDPR, processing is lawful if it is necessary for the purposes of the legitimate interests pursued by the controller or a third party, except where such interests are overridden by the fundamental rights and freedoms of the data subject. So, the EU also utilizes a balancing process comparing the controller's interest versus the data subject's interest. When looking at hotel skippers—individuals who are arguably doing something immoral or illegal—I suspect the balancing exercise would still tilt in favour of the hotels. The student countered this by asking if you could argue that these individuals risk having no place to stay if every hotel bans them, which is a significant harm. While you could certainly make that argument during the balancing test, the hotels would likely respond that the individuals brought that consequence upon themselves by regularly failing to pay their fees.  

    一位学生针对酒店逃单者的例子提出了一个很好的问题，想知道这种做法在欧盟 GDPR 下是否会有不同的评判，或者在那里是否也能被接受。我解释说，在欧盟，也有合法利益的概念，但它的框架略有不同。在新加坡，合法利益仅仅是同意义务的一个例外；默认规则是组织应该获得同意，除非有正当理由不能这样做。在其他司法管辖区，如欧盟 GDPR，合法利益是与同意并列的独立法律依据。根据欧盟 GDPR 第 6 条，如果处理是为了控制者或第三方追求的合法利益所必需的，那么处理就是合法的，除非这些利益被数据主体的基本权利和自由所凌驾。因此，欧盟也使用一个平衡过程，比较控制者的利益与数据主体的利益。当审视酒店逃单者——那些可以说正在做一些不道德或非法事情的个人——时，我怀疑平衡测试仍然会向酒店倾斜。该学生对此提出反驳，问是否可以说，如果所有酒店都封杀他们，这些人就会面临无处可住的风险，这是一种重大伤害。虽然你当然可以在平衡测试中提出这个论点，但酒店可能会回应说，这些人是因为经常不缴费而咎由自取。

  - [18:53:27 - 18:59:09] **Student Q&A: Assessing Harms and Defining Legitimate Scope**

    Another student asked how an organisation is supposed to objectively assess the balance of the aim versus the harm. They brought up a real-world case from the US involving a company called "Flock Safety" (referred to by the student as Black Cameras) that sells license plate data to police. The student noted that people view this as a massive privacy invasion—especially regarding controversial issues like tracking individuals crossing state lines for abortions—but the company claims a legitimate interest in public safety. I acknowledged that this sort of balancing is notoriously uncertain because we usually cannot assign a clear monetary value to each competing interest; it ends up being a somewhat impressionistic assessment. While the US relies heavily on constitutional law principles to conduct this sort of balancing, we do not have those exact counterparts in Singapore's privacy framework. Furthermore, the Flock Safety situation is complex because it involves a private-public relationship (selling to the government), and the Singapore public sector is largely exempted from the PDPA. Ultimately, the organisation has to make a judgment call. It is only safe to rely on this exception when the scale is very heavily tilted in your favour. If the balance is borderline, it is unsafe because the PDPC might ultimately disagree with your assessment. The student posited that a public safety benefit for a large number of people might automatically outweigh the punishment of a few. I warned against treating it purely as a numerical exercise. A speculative, slight benefit to a massive public cannot easily outweigh a severe, immediate harm to a few individuals. Another student asked how common it is for companies to actually rely on this exception. I noted that while there are no comprehensive studies—since reliance isn't widely publicised—the general position of the Singapore regulator is that if you *can* rely on consent, you *should*. This exception is best used when consent is an inappropriate mechanism. Finally, a student asked if legitimate interests are strictly limited to preventing immoral or illegal activities. I clarified that there is no strict limit. For example, in a past case involving RedMart (the online grocery company), the legitimate interest was ensuring food safety—making sure groceries were hygienic and untampered with. What counts as legitimate truly depends on the specific activity.

    另一位学生问，组织应该如何客观地评估目标与伤害之间的平衡。他们提到了美国的一个真实案例，涉及一家名为“Flock Safety”（该学生称之为 Black Cameras）的公司，该公司向警方出售车牌数据。该学生指出，人们认为这是对隐私的严重侵犯——尤其是在追踪跨州堕胎等有争议的问题上——但该公司声称这符合公共安全的合法利益。我承认这种权衡出了名的不确定，因为我们通常无法为每一个相互竞争的利益赋予明确的货币价值；它最终变成了一种有些印象式的评估。虽然美国严重依赖宪法原则来进行这种平衡，但在新加坡的隐私框架中我们没有完全相同的对应物。此外，Flock Safety 的情况很复杂，因为它涉及公私关系（卖给政府），而新加坡公共部门在很大程度上不受 PDPA 的约束。最终，组织必须做出自己的判断。只有当天平严重向你倾斜时，依赖这个例外才是安全的。如果平衡处于边缘状态，那就是不安全的，因为 PDPC 最终可能会不同意你的评估。该学生认为，对大量人群的公共安全益处可能自动超过对少数人的惩罚。我警告大家不要把它纯粹当成数字游戏。对庞大公众来说，推测性的、轻微的益处，不能轻易抵消对少数个人造成的严重、直接的伤害。另一位学生问，公司实际上依赖这种例外的情况有多普遍。我指出，虽然没有全面的研究——因为依赖该例外的情况并未被广泛宣传——但新加坡监管机构的总体立场是，如果你*能*依赖同意，你就*应该*去获取同意。当同意是一种不适当的机制时，最好使用这个例外。最后，一位学生问合法利益是否严格限于防止不道德或非法活动。我澄清说，没有严格的限制。例如，在过去一个涉及 RedMart（在线杂货公司）的案例中，合法利益是确保食品安全——确保杂货卫生且未被篡改。什么算作合法，完全取决于具体的活动。

    Let's consider a realistic, though hypothetical, example designed to align with reality. We have RHC, a ride-hailing company like Uber or Grab. This cuts RHC out as the middleman, meaning RHC doesn't earn its usual fee. Although the drivers are independent contractors and not employees, RHC strictly prohibits users of the BARG app from making these off-platform arrangements through their terms and conditions. Because it is challenging to detect these arrangements, RHC decides to use an algorithmic tool that will automatically track and analyze the location data of its drivers. Crucially, this location data is originally collected by the app for the purpose of passenger safety. RHC wants to use this data now to detect suspicious driving patterns—for example, if a driver makes a repeat journey every day from point A to point B with no booking in the system. Where the algorithmic tool assesses that there is an 80% probability of an off-platform arrangement, RHC will automatically expel the driver from the BARG app. The question for you is: Do you think RHC can rely on the legitimate interest exception to process this data? I want you to take a few minutes to think about this question and work through the three assessment steps we discussed. Specifically, think about how you would articulate the interest and how you would evaluate the adverse effects given the 80% probability threshold. We will take a few minutes and then come back together to discuss.  

    让我们考虑一个现实的、尽管是假设的、旨在与现实相符的例子。我们有一家像 Uber 或 Grab 一样的网约车公司 RHC。他们运营着“BARG”应用程序，将乘客与司机连接起来。最近几个月，RHC 注意到其收入下降。他们收到传闻，说一些司机正在进行平台外交易，这意味着司机绕过应用程序直接与乘客进行交易。这把 RHC 这个中间人踢出局，意味着 RHC 赚不到平时的费用。尽管司机是独立承包商而非雇员，但 RHC 通过其条款和条件严格禁止 BARG 应用程序的用户进行这些平台外交易。因为很难发现这些交易，RHC 决定使用一种算法工具，该工具将自动跟踪和分析其司机的位置数据。至关重要的是，这些位置数据最初是应用程序出于乘客安全的目的而收集的。RHC 现在想使用这些数据来检测可疑的驾驶模式——例如，如果一个司机每天都在 A 点和 B 点之间重复行驶，而系统中却没有预订。如果算法工具评估存在 80% 的平台外交易概率，RHC 将自动把该司机从 BARG 应用程序中开除。你们要思考的问题是：你们认为 RHC 能否依赖合法利益例外来处理这些数据？我希望你们花几分钟时间思考这个问题，并完成我们讨论过的三个评估步骤。具体来说，思考一下你们将如何阐明利益，以及考虑到 80% 的概率阈值，你们将如何评估不利影响。我们将花几分钟时间，然后重新集合进行讨论。

    Alright, let's come back and build a record of how you would conduct this assessment. Who wants to start by articulating the legitimate interest? One student suggested that the legitimate interest is protecting the commercial revenue, which is a fundamental business interest. Another student interjected, arguing that stating "protecting the business" is too broad and therefore not a valid legitimate interest. I agreed that framing it merely as "growing revenue" is likely too non-specific to qualify as an articulated benefit. Every profit-making business wants to grow revenue. The specific legitimate interest RHC is trying to achieve here is to eliminate malicious off-platform arrangements that violate their terms of service, which in turn boosts and protects their revenue. Some students suggested framing the interest around passenger safety or preventing overcharging. While you could make those arguments, there is a question mark over whether this level of control over independent contractor drivers—primarily driven by revenue protection—would withstand regulatory scrutiny as a legitimate interest. If you want to articulate a legitimate interest, you should state a benefit that is specific and believable, rather than a generic claim of wanting to increase profits.

    好了，让我们重新集合，记录下你们将如何进行这项评估。谁想先来阐明一下合法利益？一位学生建议，合法利益是保护商业收入，这是一项基本的商业利益。另一位学生插话反驳，认为仅仅陈述“保护业务”过于宽泛，因此不是有效的合法利益。我同意，仅仅将其表述为“增加收入”可能过于笼统，不符合阐明利益的标准。每个盈利性企业都想增加收入。RHC 在这里试图实现的具体合法利益是，消除违反其服务条款的恶意平台外交易，从而促进并保护其收入。一些学生建议围绕乘客安全或防止超额收费来构建利益。虽然你可以提出这些论点，但对于独立承包商司机施加如此程度的控制（主要是受收入保护驱动）是否能经得起监管机构作为合法利益的审查，仍存有疑问。如果你想阐明合法利益，你应该陈述一个具体且可信的益处，而不是笼统地声称想要增加利润。

  - [19:13:41 - 19:17:21] **Debrief: Alternatives and Purpose Limitation**

    A student raised an excellent point regarding necessity, asking if the company should first consider alternative ways to prevent this before directly invading the drivers' privacy by repurposing their personal data. This logic feeds directly into the balancing exercise. If you can achieve the benefit—whether that's safety or preventing overcharging—through a different, less intrusive method (like simply mandating that drivers turn on the app to pick up messages), then the balance tips sharply against the organisation. Another student asked if RHC could just use the algorithm since they already obtained the drivers' consent to collect location data for passenger safety. I clarified that obtaining consent to collect data for purpose A (passenger safety) does not grant you the right to use that data for a completely unconsented purpose B (policing off-platform arrangements). That would violate the purpose limitation principle. The mere fact that you already possess the data doesn't circumvent the rules. A different student asked how RHC would go about disclosing this practice. To satisfy the disclosure requirement, RHC would have to update their data protection policy or put a clear notification on the app stating that they are relying on the legitimate interest exception to monitor for these off-platform arrangements.

    一位学生提出了一个关于必要性的极好观点，询问公司是否应该在为了重新利用司机的个人数据而直接侵犯他们隐私之前，首先考虑防止这种情况的替代方法。这种逻辑直接影响了平衡测试。如果你能通过一种不同的、侵入性较小的方法（比如仅仅强制司机打开应用程序来接收信息）来实现诸如安全或防止超额收费等好处，那么天平就会急剧向不利于组织的方向倾斜。另一位学生问，既然 RHC 已经获得了司机同意为了乘客安全而收集位置数据，他们是否可以直接使用这个算法。我澄清说，获得出于目的 A（乘客安全）收集数据的同意，并不赋予你将该数据用于完全未经同意的目的 B（监管平台外交易）的权利。那将违反目的限制原则。你已经拥有数据这一事实并不能规避规则。另一位学生问 RHC 将如何去披露这种做法。为了满足披露要求，RHC 必须更新他们的数据保护政策，或者在应用程序上发布明确的通知，声明他们依赖合法利益例外来监控这些平台外交易。

    Moving on to the adverse impact assessment, a student correctly pointed out that we should only focus on the harm to the individuals (the drivers), not the impact on the company if they fail to take action. The adverse effect assessment looks strictly at harms to the individuals whose personal data is being processed. A sharp point was made regarding the 80% probability threshold. Because the algorithmic action is not certain, there is a meaningful uncertainty that will result in wrong expulsions. This means a significant number of innocent drivers will be kicked off the platform, losing their livelihood, which is a massive adverse effect. The student acutely noted that the difficulty in defining what constitutes an "off-platform arrangement" worsens this harm. For example, a driver might just be ferrying a family member or participating in an extended family carpool based on social norms. An automated expulsion for these benign actions, without review, is a severe, unjustified harm. This high likelihood of expelling the wrong drivers is a critical adverse effect that must be accounted for. Ultimately, multiple students concluded that the adverse effects in this scenario are far too strong, meaning RHC cannot implement this system in its current, automated form. The balance is entirely against the organisation.

    接着进行不利影响评估，一位学生正确地指出，我们应该只关注对个人（司机）的伤害，而不是公司如果不采取行动所受到的影响。不利影响评估严格审视对其个人数据正在被处理的个人的伤害。有人针对 80% 的概率阈值提出了一个尖锐的观点。因为算法行动不是绝对确定的，这种有意义的不确定性将导致错误的开除。这意味着大量无辜的司机将被踢出平台，失去生计，这是一个巨大的不利影响。该学生敏锐地指出，定义什么构成“平台外交易”的困难加剧了这种伤害。例如，司机可能只是在接送家人，或者基于社会规范参与大家庭的拼车。对这些善意行为进行未经审查的自动开除，是一种严重且不合理的伤害。这种极有可能开除错误司机的情况，是一个必须考虑的关键不利影响。最终，多名学生得出结论，在这种情况下，不利影响太强烈了，这意味着 RHC 无法以目前这种自动化的形式实施这个系统。平衡结果完全不利于组织。
