# Seminar 5 (gemini)
## Week 5 Summary
- **Access Obligation 访问义务**
  - **Core Principle:** Organizations must provide individuals with their personal data and information on how it was used or disclosed within the past year. No reason is required from the requester, but organizations must verify their identity.
  - **Timeframe & Fees:** Respond "as soon as reasonably possible" (generally within 30 days). Organizations may charge a reasonable fee for the incremental costs of providing access (e.g., printing costs, specific manpower), but never for capital purchases (e.g., buying new equipment) or regular operating expenses.
  - **Permissive Exceptions:** Organizations *may* refuse access for "opinion data" kept solely for an evaluative purpose (e.g., employment references, proprietary credit-scoring algorithms). They may also refuse if the request is unreasonable (repetitious, frivolous, or disproportionately burdensome).
  - **Prohibitive Exceptions:** Organizations *must not* provide access if it would reveal another individual's personal data, unless that data is publicly available, consent is given, or it is required by other laws. Partial access (redacting third-party data) must be provided if technically possible.
  - **核心原则：** 组织必须向个人提供其个人数据，以及过去一年内该数据被使用或披露的信息。请求者无需提供理由，但组织必须核实其身份。
  - **时间期限与费用：** 必须在“合理可行的情况下尽快”回复（通常在 30 天内）。组织可以收取合理的费用以支付提供访问权限的增量成本（如打印费、特定人工费），但绝不能收取资本购买（如购买新设备）或常规运营开支的费用。
  - **允许性例外：** 如果是仅出于“评估目的”而保留的“意见数据”（例如就业推荐信、专有的信用评分算法），组织“可以”拒绝访问。如果请求不合理（重复、无理取闹或负担不成比例），也可以拒绝。
  - **禁止性例外：** 如果提供访问会泄露另一人的个人数据，组织“绝对不能”提供，除非该数据已公开、已获得同意或法律要求。如果技术上可行，必须提供部分访问权限（涂黑第三方数据）。
- **Correction Obligation 纠正义务**
  - **Core Principle & Workflow:** Individuals can request corrections to errors or omissions in their personal data. Organizations must update their records and send the corrected data to any third-party organizations that received the raw data within the past year (unless the third party no longer needs it for legal/business purposes).
  - **Timeframe & Fees:** Corrections must be made as soon as practicable (generally 30 days). Crucially, organizations are strictly prohibited from charging a fee for processing a correction request.
  - **Rejection Grounds & Exceptions:** Requests can be rejected if the organization has "reasonable grounds" to believe the data is already accurate (in which case, the data must be annotated). Organizations do not have to correct subjective "opinion data" or "derived personal data" (insights generated internally by analyzing other data).
  - **核心原则与流程：** 个人可以请求纠正其个人数据中的错误或遗漏。组织必须更新其记录，并将纠正后的数据发送给在过去一年内接收过原始数据的任何第三方组织（除非第三方出于法律/商业目的不再需要它）。
  - **时间期限与费用：** 必须在切实可行的情况下尽快进行纠正（通常为 30 天）。至关重要的是，严禁组织为处理纠正请求收取费用。
  - **拒绝理由与例外情况：** 如果组织有“合理理由”认为数据已经准确，可以拒绝请求（在此情况下，必须对数据进行批注）。组织不需要纠正主观的“意见数据”或“衍生个人数据”（通过分析其他数据在内部生成的见解）。
- **Comparative Privacy Rights 比较隐私权**
  - **Data Portability:** The right to receive data in a machine-readable format and transmit it directly to another organization. Enacted in Singapore but not yet in force due to major challenges regarding format standardization, security risks during transit, and heavy compliance costs for small businesses.
  - **Right to Erasure (Right to be Forgotten):** Prominent in the EU (GDPR), allowing individuals to have data deleted or delisted from search engines (e.g., the Google Spain case). This right does *not* exist under Singapore's PDPA.
  - **数据可携权：** 以机器可读格式接收数据并直接传输给另一个组织的权利。在新加坡已立法但尚未生效，原因是面临格式标准化、传输过程中的安全风险以及小企业合规成本过高等重大挑战。
  - **删除权（被遗忘权）：** 在欧盟（GDPR）中非常突出，允许个人要求删除数据或从搜索引擎中下架（如 Google Spain 案）。这项权利在新加坡的 PDPA 下“不”存在。
## Week 5 Learning Tips
Welcome to another exciting week of mastering data protection! As we dive into the Access and Correction obligations, my biggest piece of advice is to avoid treating data governance as merely a bureaucratic compliance checklist. Instead, build your intuition around the "why" of these frameworks. The PDPA is designed to strike a delicate balance between empowering individual autonomy and ensuring businesses can practically function. When you see a rule—like why you can charge a fee for access but never for correction—think about the underlying incentive structure. Access requires heavy operational lifting (gathering, filtering, redacting), hence a reasonable incremental fee is allowed. Correction, however, fixes a company's own flawed database, which benefits both the user and the company's data integrity, so it must be free.

欢迎来到激动人心的又一周数据保护学习之旅！随着我们深入探讨访问和纠正义务，我给大家最大的建议是：不要仅仅将数据治理视为官僚主义的合规检查清单。相反，要围绕这些框架背后的“原因”来培养你的直觉。PDPA 的设计初衷是为了在赋予个人自主权和确保企业能够实际运作之间取得微妙的平衡。当你看到一条规则时——比如为什么你可以为访问请求收费，但绝不能为纠正请求收费——思考一下背后的激励结构。访问权限需要繁重的操作（收集、过滤、涂黑），因此允许收取合理的增量费用。然而，纠正是修复公司自身存在缺陷的数据库，这既有利于用户，也有利于公司的数据完整性，所以它必须是免费的。

To master the various exceptions (permissive vs. prohibitive), practice analyzing the conflicting interests at play. A common pitfall is assuming individuals have absolute reign over anything containing their name. Remember the distinction between pure factual data and "opinion" or "derived" data. If an algorithm generates a credit score or a professor writes an admission reference, that is protected evaluative data. You cannot force a company to change its internal analytical conclusions just because you disagree with them. When studying these policies, always create mini-scenarios in your head: Ask yourself, "If a customer demands X, what competing right (like third-party privacy or business proprietary logic) gives the organization the legal shield to say no?"

为了掌握各种例外情况（允许性例外与禁止性例外），请练习分析其中起作用的利益冲突。一个常见的误区是，认为个人对任何包含其名字的事物都拥有绝对的控制权。请记住纯事实数据与“意见”或“衍生”数据之间的区别。如果算法生成了信用评分，或者教授写了入学推荐信，那就是受保护的评估数据。你不能仅仅因为你不同意，就强迫公司改变其内部的分析结论。在学习这些政策时，请始终在脑海中构建微型场景：问问自己，“如果客户要求 X，什么相互竞争的权利（如第三方隐私或企业专有逻辑）为组织提供了可以说不的法律盾牌？”
## Week 5 Lecture
  - [18:38:37 - 18:41:30] **Introduction to the Access Obligation and Identity Verification**

    In addition to the previously discussed obligations, an individual is also entitled to request information about how their personal data has been used or disclosed. Specifically, an individual can request that the organisation inform them about how their personal data was used or disclosed within the past year. This constitutes the Access Obligation. So, how is an organisation supposed to deal with an access request? There are a few key pointers we can draw from the guidelines. The first thing to understand is that the individual does not need to provide a reason for the request. They can ask for access for any reason whatsoever and are under no duty to inform the organisation why they want their personal data. It could well be that the individual simply wants to hold the organisation accountable, or it could be for a secondary personal reason. A very common reason that arises is when an individual is engaging in legal proceedings or trying to investigate an incident, and it so happens the organisation has a security camera that recorded it. The individual will then ask the organisation to show them the footage. That being said, while the individual isn't required to give a reason, the organisation can certainly ask for one. The reason an organisation might want to do this is because it helps figure out exactly what the individual is looking for. The individual might actually want something quite specific, and knowing the reason allows the organisation to narrow down the scope of records they need to produce, which makes the job much easier. The second crucial point is that when an organisation receives an access request, they must undertake due diligence to verify the identity of the person making the request. You have to ensure that the person asking for the personal data is exactly who they claim to be. If someone is asking for Person A's personal data, you must make sure that the requester is indeed Person A, or someone who is legally authorised to stand in for them.

    除了之前讨论过的义务，个人还有权要求了解其个人数据是如何被使用或披露的。具体来说，个人可以要求组织告知他们，在过去一年内，其个人数据是如何被使用或被披露的。这就是“访问义务”（Access Obligation）。那么，组织应该如何处理访问请求呢？从指南中我们可以看到几个关键点。首先要明白的是，个人在提出请求时，不需要提供理由。他们可以出于任何原因要求访问数据，并且没有义务告诉组织他们为什么要这些数据。这很可能是因为个人想要追究组织的责任，或者是因为一些次要的个人原因。一个非常常见的例子是，个人目前正在参与某些法律诉讼，或者试图调查发生的一起事件，而刚好组织的安全摄像头记录下了这个事件。于是，个人就会要求组织向他们展示这段录像。话虽如此，尽管个人不需要提供理由，但组织当然可以主动询问原因。组织之所以想这么做，是因为如果不问清楚，就很难弄清楚个人到底想要什么。个人可能实际上想要非常具体的东西，如果知道原因，组织就可以缩小需要提供的数据范围，从而减轻工作量。第二个关键点是，当组织收到访问请求时，必须进行尽职调查，以核实提出请求的人的身份。你必须确保要求获取数据的人确实是他们自称的那个人。如果有人要求获取“A先生”的个人数据，你必须确保提出请求的人就是“A先生”本人，或者是合法代表他的人。

  - [18:41:46 - 18:43:27] **Methods of Providing Access**

    Let us say the organisation has decided that they have to give access to this individual. How exactly is this access supposed to be granted? The Personal Data Protection Act (PDPA) does not actually specify rigid rules on how you must provide access. It leaves this somewhat open, which means the organisation has some flexibility in determining the method. The most common method for providing access is simply by giving the individual a copy of the document. If they ask for a specific record, you provide a copy of that record. However, in some cases, that may not be viable. For example, it might not be easy to duplicate or create a copy of the specific personal data. Alternatively, the personal data might be embedded within a highly confidential document, and you do not want a physical or digital copy of that document floating around. In such cases, the organisation could suggest that the individual come down in person to examine the data. That also counts as providing access. A third example is if the organisation maintains an online portal that the individual already has access to; the organisation can simply provide the requested data through that portal. My point here is that there is no strict limit on the format or method of how you provide access.

    假设组织已经决定必须向该个人提供访问权限。那么，具体应该如何授予这种访问权限呢？《个人数据保护法》（PDPA）实际上并没有具体规定你必须如何提供访问权限。它留有一定的余地，这意味着组织在提供访问的方式上有一定的灵活性。提供访问权限最常见的方法，就是直接给个人一份文件的副本。如果个人要求提供某份文件，你就提供那份文件的副本。然而，在某些情况下，这可能是行不通的。例如，复制或创建某些个人数据的副本可能并不容易。或者出于某种原因，这些个人数据刚好在一份相当机密的文件中，而你不希望这份文件的副本被传播出去。在这种情况下，组织也可以建议个人亲自到现场来查看个人数据。这也算作提供了访问权限。第三个例子是，如果组织维护着一个个人可以登录的在线门户网站，那么组织就可以直接通过该门户网站向他们提供数据。我的意思是，关于如何提供访问权限，法律并没有严格的限制。

  - [18:43:27 - 18:45:18] **Disclosing the Use and Disclosure of Personal Data**

    That covers the first aspect—when an individual asks for access to their actual personal data. The second aspect of this obligation deals with when the individual wants to find out how the organisation has used or disclosed their personal data over the past year. There are specific guidelines for this as well. Number one: when you are informing the individual about how you used their personal data, you do not have to state the exact, specific activities or dates you engaged in. For example, if you used their personal data for an internal audit, you can simply say, "We used your personal data for audit purposes." You do not have to say, "We conducted an internal audit on 13 May 2025, and that is when we used your data." In other words, you don't have to be overly detailed regarding the internal use of the data. However, when it comes to disclosure—meaning the individual wants to know how you shared their personal data with external parties—you do need to be highly specific. You must tell the individual exactly which third parties you sent their personal data to. For instance, if you disclosed their personal data to XYZ Pharmacy, you must explicitly state, "I disclosed your personal data to XYZ Pharmacy." You cannot just use a broad, general category and say, "I disclosed your personal data to pharmaceutical companies."

    [Image: Diagram comparing 'Data Use' (internal, general purpose like 'Audit') vs 'Data Disclosure' (external, specific third party like 'XYZ Pharmacy')]

    这涵盖了第一个方面——即个人要求访问其具体的个人数据时。这项义务的第二个方面，是当个人想了解组织在过去一年中是如何“使用”或“披露”其个人数据时的规定。关于这一点也有具体的指南。第一，当你要告诉个人你如何使用他们的个人数据时，你不需要说明你使用数据时的具体、确切的活动细节。例如，如果你刚好出于内部审计的目的使用了该个人数据，你只需说：“我们出于审计目的使用了您的个人数据。”你不需要具体到说：“我们在2025年5月13日进行了内部审计，就在那时我们使用了您的数据。”换句话说，在提供有关如何使用个人数据的信息时，你不需要事无巨细。然而，当涉及到披露时，也就是说，如果个人想知道你是如何向外披露他们的个人数据的，你就必须非常具体了。这意味着你必须准确地告诉个人你把他们的数据发送给了谁。你必须指出具体的第三方。例如，如果你将个人数据披露给了“XYZ药房”，那么你必须明确告诉个人：“我将个人数据披露给了XYZ药房。”你不能仅仅用一个笼统的类别，敷衍地说：“我将您的个人数据披露给了制药公司。”

  - [18:45:33 - 18:48:31] **Response Timeframes, Fees, and Data Preservation**

    When you receive an access request, the first practical question is: how much time do you have to provide the access? The simple answer in the law is that you must respond "as soon as reasonably possible." It doesn't give a strict statutory deadline out of the gate. However, what the PDPC (Personal Data Protection Commission) has stated in its guidelines is that you are generally expected to reply within 30 days. If you cannot fulfill the request within 30 days, then you need to write to the individual and inform them exactly how long you will actually need to respond. In most standard cases, I think 30 days is a fairly generous amount of time if we are being honest. The next major question is: can you charge the individual a fee for processing the access request? The answer is yes, you can charge a fee. But the fee should be negligible. You absolutely cannot use access requests as a profit-making mechanism. You are only allowed to charge the individual for the incremental costs incurred in responding to that specific access request. You definitely cannot charge an individual for capital purchases. For instance, if you have to purchase a new machine to comply with the access request, you cannot pass the price of that machine onto the individual. If you do intend to charge a fee, you are required to give the individual a written estimate of how much the fee will be beforehand. Finally, there is a very important rule that organisations sometimes block or forget, whether deliberately or accidentally. When you are currently processing an access request—meaning you haven't given access yet, you're trying to decide whether to grant it, or you're simply trying to locate the personal data—you must take reasonable steps to preserve that personal data. This rule is particularly crucial when it comes to personal data stored on systems that automatically overwrite themselves. The classic example is security camera footage, which might overwrite itself with new data every week. If you receive an access request for such footage, your immediate first step must be to preserve it—for example, by saving a separate copy of the requested timeframe. You must ensure that the data doesn't get automatically erased or overwritten by the time you finally make the decision to grant access.

    当您收到访问请求时，第一个实际问题是，您有多长时间来提供访问权限？法律中的直接答案是，您必须“在合理可能的情况下尽快”做出回应。它并没有一开始就给出一个严格的法定截止日期。但是，PDPC（个人数据保护委员会）在指南中说明的是，通常期望您在30天内回复。如果您无法在30天内回复，那么您需要书面通知该个人，告诉他们您实际还需要多长时间。老实说，在大多数情况下，我认为30天是一个相当充裕的时间。下一个重要问题是，您可以向个人收取处理访问请求的费用吗？答案是可以。您可以收取费用，但这个费用应该是微不足道的。您绝对不能把这当成一种盈利机制。您只能向个人收取响应该特定访问请求所产生的“增量成本”。您绝对不能向个人收取资本购买的费用。例如，如果您为了满足访问请求而购买了一台新机器，您不能把机器的价格算在个人头上。如果您打算向个人收取费用，那么您需要事先给个人一份关于费用预估的书面通知。最后，这是一条组织有时会有意或无意忽略或阻碍的重要规则。在您实际处理访问请求的过程中——这意味着您还没有授予访问权限，您还在决定是否授予，或者您正在努力寻找该个人数据时——您应该采取合理的措施来保存这些个人数据。当涉及到存储在经常自动覆盖的设备上的个人数据时，这条规则尤为重要。最典型的例子就是安全摄像头的录像，它可能每一周就会被新数据覆盖。如果您收到了要求查看此类录像的访问请求，您首先要做的就是采取措施保存这些个人数据。例如，您可以把被要求的录像数据单独保存一份。您必须确保，在您最终决定提供访问权限时，这些数据不会被自动删除或覆盖。

  - [18:48:31 - 18:50:10] **Examples of Reasonable and Unreasonable Fees**

    Let's look at a couple of examples regarding fees, as this is something organisations are often concerned about. In our first example, we have an individual asking for a paper copy of his personal data. The company, Company TUV, chooses to charge a fee of $50 because the information needs to be printed out onto 50 pieces of paper. This $50 represents the incremental cost of producing that physical copy. The PDPC takes the view that this is a reasonable fee. You are basically charging for the material cost of the paper, the printing toner, and the specific labour cost of actually producing the copy. Those are true incremental costs. The second example is a negative one. Here we have Company ZYX, which receives an access request from a customer to view their personal data. However, the data is stored in an obsolete or specific format that is only readable by a certain type of machine. In this case, the company owns two such machines, but both are currently faulty. In order to respond to the customer's request, ZYX purchases a brand new machine which costs $50,000, and they choose to transfer that entire capital cost to the customer as the access fee. I think this is an obvious case where the fee is entirely unreasonable. The organisation is expected to have the general means to comply with access requests, and they cannot pass capital expenditure onto the requester.

    关于费用问题，这是组织经常关心的事情，我们来看几个例子。在第一个例子中，我们看到一个人要求提供其个人数据的纸质副本。TUV公司决定收取50美元的费用，因为这些信息需要打印在50张纸上。这50美元代表了提供这份复印件的增量成本。PDPC认为这种收费是合理的。基本上，你收取的是打印成本、纸张成本，以及实际制作复印件的人工成本。这些都属于增量成本。第二个例子是一个反面教材。ZYX公司收到了客户的访问请求，要求查看其个人数据，但这些个人数据的格式只有某种特殊机器才能读取。在这个例子中，该公司拥有两台这样的机器，但两台都坏了。为了响应客户的请求，ZYX公司购买了另一台价值5万美元的新机器，并选择将这笔费用作为“访问费”转嫁给客户。我认为这显然是一个费用不合理的例子。组织理应具备满足客户访问请求的基本手段，不能将资本支出转嫁给请求者。

  - [18:50:10 - 18:55:10] **Student Q&A: Access Fees and Third-Party API Subscriptions**

    Are there any questions before I continue?

    *Student:* "So in the second case, what should the company do if they have no intention to repair the two faulty machines, and the data cannot be read in any way?"

    *Professor:* I think this case is a bit far-fetched because realistically, there's rarely data that can only be viewed by one specific proprietary machine that can't be repaired. But if such a case did arise, the answer is that the company would either have to eat the cost of repairing it themselves, or they would have to request additional time from the individual, saying, "My machines are down. I need some time to repair them, please give me two months to process this." But you absolutely cannot charge the individual for the cost of repairing the machine or buying a new one.

    *Student:* "Then can you charge the cost of manpower?"

    *Professor:* Yes, I think manpower definitely counts as an incremental cost. If you are able to accurately calculate how many man-hours it took for a staff member to find the data and produce the copy, that counts as part of the incremental cost you can charge.

    *Student:* "Is there a price cap that you can charge?"

    *Professor:* There is no fixed pricing guideline or cap, because the general principle is that you calculate exactly how much you spent incrementally in providing access, and you charge exactly that amount.

    *Student:* "Does the example for an API access monthly subscription also come under this category?"

    *Professor:* If you were already going to pay this API subscription anyway for the regular functioning of your company, regardless of whether you are giving access or not, then you cannot charge the individual for it. That is not an incremental cost incurred by providing access. You were already paying for that subscription. Unless, of course, you are buying a specific API tier or access pass *solely* to get access to this particular user's data to fulfill their request.

    *Student:* "To give an example: I have this XYZ company. If you want to access their data, they have this API subscription, so you have to pay $100 monthly. You are developing an app that uses this API to communicate with their data. That kind of scenario."

    *Professor:* Let me rephrase the question to make sure I understand. You are a company, and you hold data that is processed or held by a third-party platform. There is an API that allows you to access that data, and you must pay a monthly fee for that API. There are two possibilities here. Possibility one: you are already paying for access to this API as part of the regular functioning of your company. In that case, you cannot charge the individual. Providing access through the API you already subscribe to does not impose an additional incremental cost. Possibility two: if it is a case where you had to purchase access to the API *specifically* and exclusively to comply with this single access request, then that could count as an incremental charge because it is an add-on expense on top of your regular business expenses just to comply with the obligation.

    *Student:* "How about instead of APIs, we get the users to pay for access to a public portal, so we can provide access to the person's request through that?"

    *Professor:* I think if directing them to a paid public portal is truly the most economical way for you to provide access, then you could tell the individual to obtain access to that portal at their own cost. However, if the company has a more economical or free internal solution to provide the data, rather than forcing the individual to pay for a portal, you would have to go with that cheaper option.

    在我继续之前，还有什么问题吗？

    *学生：*“那么在第二个案例中，如果公司根本不打算修理那两台坏掉的机器，数据也就无法以任何方式读取，公司该怎么办？”

    *教授：*我认为这种情况有点牵强，因为在现实中，很少有只能用某种特定的、无法修复的机器才能读取的数据。但如果真的出现这种情况，答案是：公司要么自己承担修理费用，要么就得向个人要求更多的时间，说明：“我的机器坏了。我需要一些时间来修理，请给我两个月的时间。”但是，你绝对不能向个人收取修理机器或购买新机器的费用。

    *学生：*“那可以收取人力成本吗？”

    *教授：*是的，我认为人力绝对算作增量成本。如果你能准确计算出员工寻找数据和制作副本花费了多少个工时，那这就算作你可以收取的增量成本的一部分。

    *学生：*“收费有上限吗？”

    *教授：*没有固定的定价指南或上限，因为一般原则是，你计算出在提供访问时额外花费了多少增量成本，你就收取那么多费用。

    *学生：*“像API访问的月度订阅费这种例子，也属于这个类别吗？”

    *教授：*如果你为了公司的正常运转，无论是否需要提供访问权限，本来就要支付这个API的订阅费，那么你就不能向个人收取这笔费用。因为这不是提供访问权限所产生的“增量成本”。无论如何你都是要付这笔订阅费的。除非，你是专门为了满足该用户的访问请求，才去购买了特定的API层级或访问权限。

    *学生：*“举个例子：我有一个XYZ公司。如果你想访问他们的数据，他们有一个API订阅，你每个月必须支付，比如100美元。你正在开发一个应用程序，使用这个API与他们的数据进行通信。大概是这种场景。”

    *教授：*让我换种说法，确保我理解了你的问题。你们是一家公司，你们的数据由某个第三方平台持有。有一个API允许你们访问这些数据，但你们必须为该API支付月费。这里有两种可能性。第一种可能：作为公司正常运作的一部分，你已经为访问这个API付费了。在这种情况下，你不能向用户收费。因为通过你已经订阅的API提供访问权限，并没有带来额外的增量成本。第二种可能：如果你是专门为了遵守这个单一的访问请求而去购买了API的访问权限，那这就可能算作增量费用，因为这是你为了履行义务而在常规业务开销之外增加的支出。

    *学生：*“如果不算API，我们让用户付费访问一个公共门户网站，通过这个网站来满足他们的访问请求，这样可以吗？”

    *教授：*我认为，如果引导他们去付费的公共门户网站确实是你们提供访问的最经济的方式，那你就可以让个人自己花钱去获取那个门户的访问权限。但是，如果公司有更经济或免费的内部解决方案来提供数据，而不是强迫个人去门户网站付费，那你就必须选择那个更便宜的选项。

  - [18:55:10 - 19:02:20] **Permissive Exceptions: Evaluative Purposes**

    Okay, so that wraps up the Access Obligation's core mechanics. Now, as with the previous obligations we've discussed, there are exceptions to the Access Obligation, meaning there are specific situations where you do not have to provide access. If you want the full list, you can find it in the PDPC guidelines or the PDPA itself, but for our purposes, we will focus on the most important ones. The exceptions are broadly divided into two types: permissive exceptions and prohibitive exceptions. Permissive exceptions mean the law allows you to choose not to give access; however, if you want to give access anyway, you are perfectly free to go ahead. Prohibitive exceptions mean you are strictly prohibited from giving access, even if you want to.

    Let me give you an important example of a permissive exception: the evaluative purpose exception. This exception states that an organisation can choose not to comply with an access request if the personal data in question is "opinion data" that is kept solely for an "evaluative purpose." This means the data isn't a hard factual "yes or no" detail; rather, it is a subjective opinion or judgment about the person. If this opinion is kept solely for an evaluative purpose, you do not have to provide access. A typical example of this would be from the university's perspective. When you apply for admission to a program, you may be asked to provide a confidential reference letter from a past teacher or employer. In that reference letter, most of the information is opinion data. It is your ex-boss's subjective judgment regarding your ability and character. This opinion data was produced and kept solely for the evaluative purpose of deciding whether you should be admitted to the university.

    What legally counts as an evaluative purpose? There is a very specific statutory list you can see on the slide. First, determining the suitability, eligibility, or qualifications of an individual for things like employment, admission to a school, awarding of bursaries and scholarships, selection for artistic/athletic purposes, or the granting of financial assistance. Second, determining whether a contract, award, or scholarship that the individual already holds should be continued, modified, or cancelled. The last one relates to deciding whether to insure an individual or property, or to renew their insurance.

    Let me give you a real-world case example involving a bank's algorithm to show how this works. The case is HSBC Bank (Singapore) Limited [2021] SGPDPC 3. An individual applied to HSBC for a credit card, and HSBC rejected the application. The individual wasn't satisfied, so he submitted an access request. This is a classic example of an access request being used not really out of concern for data protection, but because the person is unhappy with a business decision. He asked for access to HSBC's internal evaluation report that was prepared to assess his credit card application. HSBC agreed and provided the report, but they heavily redacted certain pieces of information. Specifically, they redacted data that was auto-generated by their proprietary algorithm—the internal ruleset that determines if someone is suitable for a credit card. Unsurprisingly, that redacted logic is exactly what the individual wanted to see, so he complained to the PDPC, demanding the full unredacted report. The core question was: Could HSBC legally withhold this algorithm-generated information? The PDPC ruled that the bank could rely on the evaluative purpose exception. The logic was that the data redacted was essentially "opinion data"—a subjective algorithmic judgment on the individual's creditworthiness—and it was kept solely for the evaluative purpose of determining whether to award the individual a contract for a credit card, which falls squarely within the statutory list. Therefore, HSBC was justified in redacting it.

    好了，访问义务的核心机制就讲到这里。现在，就像我们之前讨论过的其他义务一样，访问义务也有一些例外情况。也就是说，在某些特定情况下，你不需要提供访问权限。如果你想看完整的列表，可以在PDPC指南或PDPA法案本身中找到。但在本课程中，我们将重点关注几个最重要的例外。这些例外大致分为两类：“允许性”例外（permissive exceptions）和“禁止性”例外（prohibitive exceptions）。允许性例外是指法律允许你选择不提供访问权限；但是，如果你自己愿意提供，你完全可以这么做。禁止性例外则是指即使你想提供，法律也严禁你提供访问权限。

    让我给你们举一个重要的允许性例外的例子：“评估目的”例外（evaluative purpose exception）。这个例外规定，如果涉及的个人数据是为了“评估目的”而专门保留的“意见数据”（opinion data），组织就可以选择不遵从访问请求。这意味着该数据不是客观事实上的“是或否”，而是关于该个人的主观意见或判断。如果这种意见仅仅是为了评估目的而保留的，你就不需要提供访问权限。一个典型的例子是从大学的角度来看。当你申请某个专业的入学许可时，可能会被要求提供一份过去老师或雇主的保密推荐信。在那封推荐信中，大部分信息都属于“意见数据”。那是你的前老板对你的能力和品格的主观判断。这些意见数据的产生和保留，仅仅是为了评估你是否应该被大学录取这个目的。

    那么在法律上，什么算作“评估目的”呢？幻灯片上有一个非常具体的法定清单。首先，是决定个人是否适合、符合资格或具备条件从事某些活动，例如就业、入学、颁发助学金和奖学金、出于艺术/体育目的的选拔，或发放财务援助。其次，是决定是否继续、修改或取消该个人已经持有的合同、奖项或奖学金。最后一个与决定是否为个人或财产提供保险，或续保有关。

    让我给你们举一个涉及银行算法的真实案例，看看它是如何运作的。这个案例是汇丰银行（新加坡）有限公司 [2021] SGPDPC 3。一个人向汇丰银行申请信用卡，但汇丰银行拒绝了他的申请。这个人很不满意，于是提交了一份访问请求。这是一个很典型的例子：人们提出访问请求，往往不是真的出于对数据保护的关心，而是因为对某项商业决定感到不满。他要求访问汇丰银行为评估他的信用卡申请而准备的内部评估报告。汇丰银行同意并提供了报告，但他们涂黑（遮挡）了某些信息。具体来说，他们删减了由其专有算法自动生成的数据——也就是决定一个人是否适合办理信用卡的内部规则集。毫不意外，那些被涂黑的逻辑，恰恰是这个人想看的，于是他向PDPC投诉，要求提供完整无删减的报告。核心问题是：汇丰银行在法律上能扣留这些由算法生成的信息吗？PDPC裁定，银行可以依赖“评估目的例外”。理由是，被删减的数据本质上是“意见数据”——即算法对个人信用度的主观判断——而且保留该数据的唯一目的是为了评估是否授予该个人信用卡合同，这完全符合法定清单的规定。因此，汇丰银行将其涂黑是合理的。

  - [19:02:33 - 19:07:24] **Student Q&A: Insurance Claims, Partial Access, and Contractual Waivers**

    *Student:* "How about for insurance? I see 'deciding whether to insure' on the slide. Does this evaluative purpose exception also apply to evaluating insurance claims?"

    *Professor:* If you look closely at the last paragraph, it says "the purpose of deciding whether to insure any individual or property." Strictly speaking, evaluating an insurance claim—where you already have an existing insurance contract and are now claiming a payout—does not fall under any of the listed evaluative purposes. Unless the insurance company can find some other way to fit it in, or rely on a different specific exception, they generally cannot use this clause to withhold claim evaluation data.

    *Student:* "Could the company just say that the whole report is for an evaluative or investigative purpose and reject the entire thing?"

    *Professor:* No, they cannot. There is another overriding rule that dictates if it is possible for you to provide partial access, then you must provide partial access. In the HSBC case, for instance, it was possible to give access to the general facts of the report that were not covered by the exception, while redacting just the algorithm's opinion data that was covered. You must provide the document with the exempted parts removed; you cannot reject the entire document if parts of it are disclosable.

    *Student:* "In that insurance example, what if the contract you sign explicitly states that you waive your PDPA rights to access?"

    *Professor:* A contract does not allow the organisation to bypass the PDPA. The fact that the organisation has a signed contract with the individual saying they waive their rights under the PDPA is legally invalid. An individual cannot contract away their data protection rights. Otherwise, it would be far too easy for companies; every organisation would just include a standard clause in their terms of service saying, "I forego all my rights under the PDPA." As we discussed in seminar two regarding subordination, a private contract cannot allow an organisation to get out of performing its statutory data protection obligations.

    *Student:* "Do credit scores given by credit bureaus fit under this evaluative exception?"

    *Professor:* There are two parts to that answer. First, a credit score probably does fit under the evaluative purpose of deciding whether to award you a loan contract. However, there are also specific laws relating to credit bureaus and credit reporting in Singapore, like the Credit Bureau Act. Specific sectoral laws generally override the broad PDPA. If the Credit Bureau Act has specific rules about individuals accessing their credit scores, those rules take precedence.

    *学生：*“那保险方面呢？我看到幻灯片上有‘决定是否承保’。这个评估目的例外，是否也适用于评估保险理赔？”

    *教授：*如果你仔细看最后一段，上面写着“决定是否为个人或财产提供保险的目的”。严格来说，评估保险理赔——也就是你已经有了一份现有的保险合同，现在要求赔付——并不属于所列出的任何评估目的。除非保险公司能找到其他方式把它套进去，或者依赖其他特定的例外条款，否则他们通常不能以此条款为由拒绝提供理赔评估数据。

    *学生：*“公司能直接说整份报告都是为了评估或调查目的，然后全部拒绝提供吗？”

    *教授：*不，他们不能。还有另一条压倒性的规则规定：如果有可能提供部分访问权限，那么你就必须提供部分访问权限。以汇丰银行的案子为例，银行完全可以提供报告中不受例外条款保护的一般事实，同时只把受保护的算法意见数据涂黑。你必须提供删除了豁免部分的文件，如果文件中有部分内容是可以披露的，你就不能拒绝提供整份文件。

    *学生：*“在那个保险的例子中，如果你签署的合同明确规定你放弃了PDPA规定的访问权呢？”

    *教授：*合同不能允许组织绕过PDPA。组织与个人签署了一份声明放弃PDPA权利的合同，这在法律上是无效的。个人不能通过合同放弃其数据保护权利。否则，这对公司来说就太容易了；每个组织都会在他们的服务条款中加入一个标准条款：“我放弃我在PDPA下的所有权利。”正如我们在第二次研讨会上讨论“从属性”（subordination）时所讲的，私人合同不能让组织免除履行其法定数据保护义务。

    *学生：*“信用局给出的信用评分符合这个评估例外吗？”

    *教授：*这个答案有两部分。首先，信用评分可能确实符合决定是否批给你贷款合同的“评估目的”。然而，新加坡也有关于信用局和信用报告的具体法律，比如《信用局法》（Credit Bureau Act）。特定的行业法律通常会凌驾于宽泛的PDPA之上。如果《信用局法》对个人访问其信用评分有具体规定，那么这些规定具有优先权。

  - [19:07:24 - 19:12:05] **Permissive Exceptions: Unreasonable Requests**

    The second major category of permissive exceptions deals with requests that are deemed unreasonable. There are five types of unreasonable requests that you can legally choose not to respond to. The first one is if the request would unreasonably interfere with the operations of the organisation because of its repetitious or systematic nature. If the individual keeps sending the exact same access request to you, say, every fortnight for the exact same set of personal data, at some point, the organisation can simply ignore this request. Number two is where the burden or expense of providing access would be unreasonable to the organisation or disproportionate to the individual's underlying interests. This happens when you *could* technically provide access, but doing so would be tremendously expensive or troublesome. For example, imagine a shopping centre receives a request from an individual demanding to view all CCTV footage of himself recorded at that mall over the entire past year. Assume this individual visits the mall quite regularly, but he refuses to specify which exact dates or incidents he is looking for. In this scenario, expecting the shopping centre to manually review thousands of hours of footage from the past year just to find every record of him would be a completely unreasonable burden. In such a case, the organisation can reject it, citing that the burden is disproportionate to whatever vague interest the individual might have. (Of course, if the individual narrows it down and says, "Actually, I just need the footage from last Tuesday at 2 PM near this specific store," then the organisation must comply.)

    *Student:* "Going back to the earlier case where data was stored on a faulty machine. Since buying a new machine is incredibly costly, could the organisation reject the request by claiming it is an unreasonable burden or expense under this exception?"

    *Professor:* There are a couple of ways the PDPC might look at that. The more likely legal answer is that it would fall under the third type of unreasonable request on our list: "The information requested does not exist or cannot be found." Since the machine is broken, the data essentially cannot be retrieved. However, it is also entirely possible that the PDPC will take a sterner view and say that it is the organisation's own fault for failing to maintain workable access to their own data, and they might still find the company liable for not providing it.

    Moving on to the fourth type of unreasonable request: this is when the information requested is "trivial," meaning it is completely unimportant and serves no real purpose. Finally, number five is when the request is otherwise frivolous or vexatious, meaning the request is made maliciously just to make things difficult for the organisation. For example, if an individual sends an email feedback form containing their name and phone number to Organisation XYZ, and the very next day they submit a formal access request asking to see the exact personal data they just submitted in that form. They know full well what information is there because they just provided it. That is a vexatious request designed purely to waste time, and it can be legally ignored.

    第二大类允许性例外涉及被认为是不合理的请求。有五种类型的不合理请求，你在法律上可以选择不予回应。第一种是，如果该请求由于其重复性或系统性，会不合理地干扰组织的运营。如果个人一直向你发送完全相同的访问请求，比如每两周就要索取一次完全相同的个人数据集，到了某个时候，组织就可以直接忽略这种请求。第二种情况是，提供访问的负担或费用对组织来说是不合理的，或者与个人的潜在利益不成比例。当你技术上“可以”提供访问权限，但这样做会非常昂贵或麻烦时，就会出现这种情况。例如，想象一家购物中心收到了某人的请求，要求查看过去一整年里该商场拍到他的所有闭路电视录像。假设这个人经常光顾这家商场，但他拒绝具体说明他想要找哪几天或什么事件的录像。在这种情况下，指望购物中心手动审查过去一年数千小时的录像，仅仅为了找到有关他的每一条记录，这将是一个完全不合理的负担。在这种情况下，组织可以拒绝请求，理由是这种负担与个人可能拥有的任何模糊利益是不成比例的。（当然，如果个人缩小了范围，说：“其实，我只需要上周二下午2点在某家特定商店附近的录像”，那么组织就必须遵守。）

    *学生：*“回到之前数据存储在损坏机器上的例子。既然买一台新机器非常昂贵，组织能不能根据这个例外，声称这构成了不合理的负担或费用，从而拒绝请求？”

    *教授：*PDPC可能会从几个方面来看待这个问题。更有可能的法律答案是，它属于我们列表上第三种不合理的请求：“所请求的信息不存在或无法找到。”既然机器坏了，数据基本上就无法检索了。然而，PDPC也完全有可能采取更严厉的态度，认为未能保持对其自身数据的有效访问是组织自己的错，他们可能仍然会判定公司因未提供数据而承担责任。

    继续看第四种不合理的请求：这是指所请求的信息是“微不足道的”（trivial），意思是它完全不重要，没有任何实际用途。最后，第五种情况是，请求在其他方面是轻率或无理取闹的（frivolous or vexatious），意思是提出请求纯粹是出于恶意，只是为了给组织制造困难。例如，如果个人向XYZ组织发送了一份包含其姓名和电话号码的电子邮件反馈表，第二天他们就提交了一份正式的访问请求，要求查看他们刚刚在那个表格中提交的确切个人数据。他们非常清楚里面有什么信息，因为就是他们刚填的。这就是一个纯粹为了浪费时间而设计的无理请求，在法律上是可以被忽略的。

  - [19:12:05 - 19:16:47] **Prohibitive Exceptions and Partial Access**

    Now let me turn to the third major category of exceptions: the prohibitive exceptions. This means you are legally not allowed to provide access under these circumstances. This exception applies if providing access to the requester would reasonably be expected to reveal personal data about another individual. So, if you have a document that contains the personal data of Individual A, and Individual A asks for access to it, but giving them that document would inevitably reveal the personal data of Individual B, then you cannot provide access. That would be an unlawful disclosure of B's personal data.

    However, there are a few caveats where this prohibition does not apply, and you *can* provide the data. We will skip the first caveat and look at rules two, three, and four. Rule two says the prohibition does not apply if you could legally disclose Individual B's personal data without their consent anyway. This often links back to the concept of publicly available data. If B's data is already publicly available, you don't need their consent to disclose it, so it does not stop you from fulfilling A's access request. Rule three is where providing the access is explicitly permitted or required by other overriding laws. Rule four is simply when Individual B explicitly gives their consent for their personal data to be disclosed to A. If B is okay with it, you can and must give access to A.

    Let's look at a couple of examples of how this works. Let's say Betty applies to the ABC shopping centre for access to CCTV footage of herself walking through the mall. Naturally, this footage contains images of other random shoppers. Even though it reveals personal data of others, those other individuals are recorded walking in a public area, so their images are considered publicly available data. In this case, ABC can and should provide the footage to Betty because the public availability exception overrides the prohibition. Conversely, consider a situation where a children's party is being held in a private condominium function room equipped with a CCTV camera. A guest realises she has lost a personal belonging and asks the management to see the CCTV footage. Here, you cannot rely on the "publicly available" exception because a booked function room is a private venue. However, you might be able to rely on the "legitimate interest" exception to provide access without consent. The legitimate interest here is helping the person locate their lost property. You would still need to limit the risk—perhaps by letting her view the footage under supervision rather than giving her a copy—but you can and should grant access on that basis, despite other people's data being visible.

    Finally, I want to reiterate a point regarding partial access. If it is practically possible for the organisation to provide access to the individual's personal data *without* revealing the other individual's personal data—usually by redacting or removing the exempted parts—then the organisation must do that. For example, Mary requests that her travel agency furnish a document confirming her flight was cancelled for an insurance claim. The official letter from the airline contains Mary's details, but it also lists the personal details of 36 other passengers on that flight. The travel agency cannot deny Mary's request entirely. They must provide partial access by blacking out or redacting the personal data of the other 36 passengers before handing the letter to Mary.

    现在让我讲讲第三大类例外情况：禁止性例外。这意味着在这些情况下，法律禁止你提供访问权限。如果向请求者提供访问权限，有理由认为会泄露另一个人的个人数据，则适用此例外情况。因此，如果你有一份包含个人A个人数据的文件，而A要求访问该文件，但将文件交给他们不可避免地会泄露个人B的个人数据，那么你就不能提供访问权限。那将构成对B个人数据的非法披露。

    然而，有几个前提条件可以使这项禁令不适用，你“可以”提供数据。我们将跳过第一个条件，直接看规则二、三和四。规则二指出，如果不管怎样，你都能在未经个人B同意的情况下合法披露其个人数据，那么该禁令就不适用。这通常与公开可用数据的概念有关。如果B的数据已经公开，你披露它就不需要经过同意，因此这不会妨碍你履行A的访问请求。规则三是指其他具有约束力的法律明确允许或要求提供访问权限。规则四很简单，就是当个人B明确同意将其个人数据披露给A时。如果B没意见，你可以且必须给予A访问权限。

    让我们看几个了解其运作方式的例子。假设Betty向ABC购物中心申请访问她自己在商场里走动的闭路电视录像。理所当然地，这段录像包含了其他路人顾客的图像。尽管它泄露了其他人的个人数据，但这些其他个人是在公共区域被拍到的，因此他们的图像被认为是公开可用的数据。在这种情况下，ABC可以也应该向Betty提供录像，因为公开可用例外优先于禁令。相反，设想这样一种情况：在配备了闭路电视摄像头的私人公寓多功能厅里正在举行一场儿童派对。一位客人发现自己丢失了私人物品，要求管理处查看闭路电视录像。在这里，你不能依赖“公开可用”例外，因为预订的多功能厅是私人场所。但是，你可能能够依赖“合法利益”例外来在未经同意的情况下提供访问。这里的合法利益是帮助该人寻找遗失物品。你仍然需要限制风险——也许是让她在监督下观看录像，而不是直接给她一份副本——但你可以也应该在此基础上授予访问权限，尽管会看到其他人的数据。

    最后，我想重申一下关于部分访问权限的观点。如果组织在实际操作中，可以在“不”泄露其他个人数据的情况下，向请求者提供其本人的个人数据——通常是通过涂黑或删除豁免部分——那么组织就必须这么做。例如，Mary要求她的旅行社提供一份确认她的航班被取消的文件，以便进行保险理赔。航空公司发出的官方信件中包含了Mary的详细信息，但也列出了该航班上其他36名乘客的个人详细信息。旅行社不能完全拒绝Mary的请求。他们必须提供部分访问权限，即在将信件交给Mary之前，涂黑或删减其他36名乘客的个人数据。

  - [19:16:47 - 19:17:24] **Procedures for Rejecting Access Requests**

    Let me turn briefly to what happens if you assess the situation and decide you are going to reject the access request. The protocol is quite straightforward. If you are rejecting the access request, you must reply to the individual in writing, explicitly telling them the reasons why you are rejecting their request. Furthermore, for 30 days after the date of that rejection, you are legally required to preserve a complete and accurate copy of the requested personal data. This is a safeguard just in case the individual takes their complaint to the PDPC, and the PDPC overrules you and mandates that you actually have to give them access. You need to ensure the data still exists.

    我简短地讲一下，如果你对情况进行了评估，并决定拒绝访问请求，会发生什么。处理程序非常简单。如果你拒绝了访问请求，你必须以书面形式回复该个人，明确告诉他们你拒绝其请求的原因。此外，在拒绝之日后的30天内，法律要求你必须保留所请求个人数据的完整且准确的副本。这是一种保护措施，以防该个人向PDPC投诉，而PDPC推翻了你的决定，并强制要求你必须为他们提供访问权限。你需要确保数据仍然存在。

  - [19:17:24 - 19:22:13] **International Comparisons and the Data Portability Obligation**

    Just to bring in some comparative points, the right to access is a very common obligation in other global jurisdictions. For example, in the EU's GDPR, Article 15 states that the data subject shall have the right to obtain confirmation from the controller as to whether their personal data is being processed, and access to that data along with the purposes of processing. This is functionally very similar to our Access Obligation. Similarly, in India, Section 11 of the DPDPA states that the data principal (the individual) has the right to obtain a summary of their personal data from the data fiduciary (the organisation) and details of the processing activities.

    In addition to access, other jurisdictions also incorporate what is known as a separate right of "data portability." The EU GDPR is the primary example here. Article 20 of the GDPR says that the data subject has the right to receive their personal data in a structured, commonly used, and machine-readable format, and crucially, they have the right to transmit that data to another controller. This is fundamentally different from the Access Obligation. The Access Obligation just gives the data to the user. Data portability requires the organisation to transmit the personal data directly to a completely different organisation, in a common machine-readable format, to facilitate the user transferring their services elsewhere.

    Singapore actually did enact a Data Portability Obligation within the PDPA, but interestingly, the government chose not to let it come into force yet. It is written on the books, but it has not been officially implemented. I will introduce you to it briefly, though you do not really have to know the deep technicalities of it for the purposes of this course. Under Section 26G of the PDPA, the stated purpose of this obligation is twofold. First, to provide individuals with more autonomy and control over their personal data. Second, to facilitate a more innovative and intensive use of data to help organisations improve their products and processes. The mechanics are laid out in Section 26H: an individual can submit a "data porting request" to a "porting organisation," instructing them to transmit "applicable data" directly to a "receiving organisation." What is highly interesting about how this provision was drafted is that the definitions of what constitutes a porting organisation, a receiving organisation, and applicable data were left deliberately open. Singapore has not yet decided on the exact scope of this obligation—whether it will apply globally to every organisation in Singapore, or if it will be confined to specific highly-regulated sectors like banking or insurance. This uncertainty is the primary reason why it hasn't been brought into force yet.

    为了引入一些比较的观点，访问权在全球其他司法管辖区也是一项非常常见的义务。例如，在欧盟的GDPR第15条中规定，数据主体有权从控制者处获得关于其个人数据是否正在被处理的确认，并有权访问这些数据以及处理目的。这在功能上与我们的访问义务非常相似。同样，在印度的DPDPA第11条中规定，数据主体（即个人）有权从数据受托人（即组织）处获取其个人数据的摘要以及处理活动的详细信息。

    除了访问权，其他司法管辖区还包含了一项独立的“数据可携权”（data portability）。欧盟的GDPR就是主要例子。GDPR第20条规定，数据主体有权以结构化、常用且机器可读的格式接收其个人数据，最关键的是，他们有权将这些数据传输给另一个控制者。这在本质上与访问义务不同。访问义务只是把数据给用户。而数据可携权要求组织直接将个人数据以通用的机器可读格式传输给一个完全不同的组织，以方便用户将他们的服务转移到其他地方。

    新加坡实际上在PDPA中也制定了“数据可携义务”，但有趣的是，政府选择暂时不让它生效。它已经写在了法律条文里，但还没有正式实施。我会向你们简单介绍一下，不过在本课程中，你们不需要深入了解它的技术细节。根据PDPA第26G条，这项义务的既定目的有两个。首先，为个人提供对其个人数据更多的自主权和控制权。其次，促进数据更创新、更密集的使用，以帮助组织改进其产品和流程。具体的操作机制在第26H条中列出：个人可以向“移出组织”（porting organisation）提交“数据携号转网请求”，指示他们直接将“适用数据”（applicable data）传输给“接收组织”（receiving organisation）。这项规定起草过程中非常有趣的一点是，关于什么是移出组织、接收组织以及适用数据的定义，被故意留下了开放的解释空间。新加坡尚未决定这项义务的确切范围——它是会广泛适用于新加坡的每一个组织，还是仅限于银行业或保险业等受高度监管的特定行业。这种不确定性是它至今尚未生效的主要原因。

  - [19:22:13 - 19:29:50] **Class Discussion: Challenges of Implementing Data Portability**

    So, one question for you all to think about: What do you think are some of the major challenges posed by this kind of data portability obligation? What explains why Singapore is holding it back from coming into force? Do you see potential problems here?

    *Student 1:* "One problem could be that data is encrypted or locked in proprietary software formats, making it hard to transfer seamlessly."

    *Professor:* Yes, absolutely. Format incompatibility is a huge problem. Different organisations choose to hold data in particular formats and structures for good operational reasons. That data may not be easily transferable to a rival organisation. To make portability work, you almost need to force a common, standardized data format across the entire industry, which is practically very difficult.

    *Student 2:* "What about fraudulent requests? Like someone making a fake portability request to essentially siphon your funds or data to their own app, similar to issues we see with Singpass or MyInfo integrations?"

    *Professor:* That is a massive problem, and it is a problem that also affects the basic access obligation. The risk is that a bad actor fraudulently makes a data porting request and has highly sensitive personal data automatically transmitted directly to a third party that the actual individual never intended to share data with.

    *Student 3:* "Could it be a geographical issue? Once the data is ported outside of Singapore, you lose legal control over it, and that data can end up anywhere. Especially since the law says the receiving organisation can be 'operating in Singapore or elsewhere'."

    *Professor:* Exactly, that is a third major challenge, which broadly falls under the umbrella of ensuring data security. There are massive risks involved both during the transmission process and *after* the transmission. Even if the data safely reaches the intended receiving organisation, that receiving entity might not be as responsible. They might be situated in a foreign jurisdiction with significantly weaker data protection laws.

    *Student 4:* "I think authenticity is an issue. How can the receiving organisation validate the authenticity of the data being ported from the source? If data is translated from one proprietary system to another, testing it and ensuring it hasn't been corrupted during the transfer might be very tricky."

    *Professor:* Yes, ensuring data integrity during transfer is another excellent point.

    *Student 5:* "I was thinking about the definition of 'organisations', which includes individuals like sole proprietors or small-time insurance agents. If data portability is enacted across the board, the compliance costs and technical burden would be way too much for them to reasonably handle."

    *Professor:* That is a critical point regarding compliance costs. Unlike general data protection rules, which a small company or sole proprietor can reasonably manage with basic administrative hygiene, building technical infrastructure for direct data portability requires serious IT investment. The average small company simply cannot afford to build automated data-porting pipelines. That economic burden is definitely one of the main reasons Singapore is holding back on broad implementation.

    *Student 6:* "Is there a reason why Singapore didn't just include the EU GDPR language that requires data to be provided in a 'structured, commonly used and machine-readable format'? Wouldn't dictating a standard language solve all the technical and format problems?"

    *Professor:* It's true that we intentionally did not include that language in our act. Why we didn't include it—if I were to speculate—is because of the immense financial concern about what that would do to businesses. If you legally mandate a "commonly used structured format," you are essentially forcing thousands of companies who currently use bespoke or legacy databases to completely overhaul their data storage architectures into a wholly new format just to comply. That would be an astronomical cost. Because of these challenges, what I suspect will end up happening is that Singapore will only implement data portability on a very narrow, sector-specific basis. They will likely roll it out only for organisations in specific sectors (like open banking or telecom) where data structures are already highly standardized, meaning there wouldn't be massive additional technical costs to comply.

    Right, let me pause here. We will take a short break, come back at 7:40, and carry on with the next obligation.

    那么，给大家留一个思考题：你们认为这种数据可携义务会带来哪些主要挑战？是什么原因让新加坡暂时搁置了它的生效？你们看出了什么潜在的问题吗？

    *学生1：*“一个问题可能是数据被加密或锁定在专有软件格式中，导致很难无缝传输。”

    *教授：*是的，绝对是这样。格式不兼容是一个巨大的问题。不同的组织出于充分的运营理由，会选择以特定的格式和结构来保存数据。这些数据可能很难转移给竞争对手组织。为了让数据可携权起作用，你几乎必须在整个行业强制推行一种通用的、标准化的数据格式，这在实际操作中是非常困难的。

    *学生2：*“那欺诈性请求呢？比如有人伪造数据可携请求，基本上就是把你的资金或数据吸收到他们自己的应用程序中，就像我们在Singpass或MyInfo集成中看到的问题一样？”

    *教授：*这是一个非常严重的问题，这也是一个同样会影响基本访问义务的问题。风险在于，恶意行为者会欺诈性地提出数据携号转网请求，并将高度敏感的个人数据自动直接传输给第三方，而个人其实根本没打算与该第三方共享数据。

    *学生3：*“这会不会是一个地理问题？一旦数据被传送到新加坡境外，你就会失去对它的法律控制，这些数据可能流落到任何地方。尤其是法律规定接收组织可以‘在新加坡或在其他地方运营’。”

    *教授：*完全正确，这是第三个主要挑战，大体上属于确保数据安全的问题。在传输过程中以及传输“之后”，都存在巨大的风险。即使数据安全到达了预期的接收组织，该接收实体也可能不如本地企业那样负责。他们可能位于数据保护法律薄弱得多的外国司法管辖区。

    *学生4：*“我认为真实性也是一个问题。接收组织如何验证从源头传出的数据的真实性？如果数据从一个专有系统转换到另一个专有系统，测试它并确保它在传输过程中没有损坏可能会非常棘手。”

    *教授：*是的，确保传输过程中的数据完整性也是一个极好的观点。

    *学生5：*“我在想‘组织’的定义，它包括了像独资经营者或小保险代理人这样的个人。如果全面实施数据可携权，合规成本和技术负担对他们来说就太重了，根本无法合理应对。”

    *教授：*这是一个关于合规成本的至关重要的观点。与一般的数据保护规则不同（小公司或独资经营者可以通过基本的行政卫生措施合理管理），为直接数据可携权建立技术基础设施需要大量的IT投资。普通的小公司根本负担不起建立自动化数据传输管道的费用。这种经济负担绝对是新加坡暂缓广泛实施的主要原因之一。

    *学生6：*“新加坡没有像欧盟GDPR那样，加入要求以‘结构化、常用且机器可读的格式’提供数据的措辞，这有什么原因吗？强制规定一种标准语言不能解决所有的技术和格式问题吗？”

    *教授：*确实，我们故意没有在法案中加入这种措辞。至于为什么没有加入——如果让我推测的话——是因为大家非常担心这会在财务上给企业带来怎样的冲击。如果你在法律上强制要求使用“常用的结构化格式”，你基本上就是迫使成千上万目前使用定制或传统数据库的公司，彻底彻底改造他们的数据存储架构，以一种全新的格式来存储数据，仅仅是为了合规。这将是一笔天文数字的成本。由于存在这些挑战，我猜测最终的情况是，新加坡只会在非常狭窄的、特定行业的基础上实施数据可携权。他们很可能只会针对特定行业（如开放银行或电信）的组织推出这项政策，因为这些行业的数据结构已经高度标准化，这意味着企业在合规方面不会产生巨大的额外技术成本。

  - [19:40:23 - 19:44:50] **Introduction to the Correction Obligation**

    This leads us to the next major requirement under the PDPA: the Correction Obligation. This obligation dictates that an individual can make a formal correction request, asking an organisation to fix any errors or omissions in the personal data that is currently in the organisation's possession or under its control. Once the organisation receives this request, it must comply and correct the personal data, unless it is satisfied on reasonable grounds that the correction should not be made. In most cases where an organisation rejects a request, the basis is that the organisation genuinely believes the personal data it already has is, in fact, accurate. But assuming the organisation accepts the request, what exactly are they supposed to do? First, they must correct the personal data in their own systems as soon as practicable. However, the obligation does not stop there. The organisation must then take the corrected personal data and send it to every other third-party organisation to which the original, incorrect data was disclosed within a one-year period prior to the correction request. Now, there is a caveat to this onward transmission rule: you do not have to send the updated data to the third party if that third party no longer needs the corrected data for any legal or business purpose. Furthermore, if you are the third-party receiving organisation that gets this corrected data, you must also carry out your own assessment and correct the personal data in your possession, unless you also have reasonable grounds not to. Let me give you a concrete example to make this flow a bit clearer. Let's say the primary organisation is an online retailer. A customer submits a request stating that their address is wrong in the system and asks for it to be updated. The retailer assesses the request, finds no reasonable grounds to reject it, assumes the customer knows their own address, and proceeds to correct the database. Having done this, the retailer then looks at who they shared this data with over the past year. They sent the address to an affiliate company responsible for servicing the customer's warranty. The retailer forwards the corrected address to this affiliate because the affiliate might need it. However, upon receiving it, the affiliate realises the customer's warranty has actually expired. Therefore, the affiliate decides they do not need the updated address for any business purpose and chooses not to make the correction in their own records. Separately, the retailer also shared the old address with a courier company a few months ago for a specific delivery. In this instance, the retailer does not even need to send the updated address to the courier company, because it was a one-off delivery; the courier company has no ongoing business need for the customer's current address.

    [Image: Diagram illustrating the Correction Obligation data flow: Individual requests correction from Retailer -> Retailer updates database -> Retailer forwards update to Affiliate (who ignores it due to expired warranty) -> Retailer skips sending to Courier (one-off delivery).]

    这就引出了PDPA规定的下一项主要要求：纠正义务（Correction Obligation）。这项义务规定，个人可以提出正式的纠正请求，要求组织修正目前在其拥有或控制下的个人数据中的任何错误或遗漏。一旦组织收到此请求，就必须遵守并纠正个人数据，除非它有合理的理由确信不应进行纠正。在组织拒绝请求的大多数情况下，理由是组织真诚地认为它已经拥有的个人数据实际上是准确的。但是，假设组织接受了请求，他们到底应该怎么做呢？首先，他们必须在切实可行的情况下尽快在其自身系统中纠正个人数据。然而，这项义务并没有到此为止。然后，组织必须将纠正后的个人数据发送给在纠正请求提出前一年内，曾接收过原始错误数据的所有第三方组织。现在，这个向下游传输的规则有一个例外：如果第三方出于任何法律或商业目的不再需要纠正后的数据，你就不必将更新后的数据发送给该第三方。此外，如果你是收到这些纠正数据的第三方接收组织，你也必须进行自己的评估，并纠正你所拥有的个人数据，除非你也有合理的理由不这样做。让我给你们举一个具体的例子，让这个流程更清晰。假设主要组织是一家在线零售商。一位客户提交了一份请求，说系统中的地址是错的，要求更新。零售商评估了该请求，没有发现拒绝的合理理由，认为客户肯定清楚自己的地址，于是就在数据库中进行了纠正。做完这些后，零售商会查看过去一年中他们与谁共享了这些数据。他们曾将该地址发送给一家负责为客户提供保修服务的关联公司。零售商将纠正后的地址转发给该关联公司，因为关联公司可能需要它。然而，关联公司收到后发现，该客户的保修实际上已经过期。因此，关联公司决定他们出于任何商业目的都不需要更新后的地址，并选择不在他们自己的记录中进行纠正。另外，几个月前，零售商还为了某次具体的配送，将旧地址分享给了一家快递公司。在这种情况下，零售商甚至不需要将更新后的地址发送给快递公司，因为那只是一次性的配送；快递公司没有持续的业务需求去了解客户当前的地址。

  - [19:44:50 - 19:47:07] **Fees, Timelines, and Exceptions for Correction**

    When it comes to the correction obligation, there is a very strict rule regarding costs: the organisation is absolutely not allowed to charge a fee for complying with a correction request. Remember, you can charge a reasonable incremental fee for an access request, but you cannot charge a fee for fixing your own records. If the organisation agrees to correct the personal data, it must do so as soon as practicable from the time of the request. Similar to the access obligation, the general regulatory guideline for a deadline is 30 days. If, for whatever reason, you cannot complete the correction within 30 days, you are required to inform the individual in writing, specifying exactly when you will be able to complete it. Now, when can an organisation reject a correction request? There are essentially two main categories of reasons. The first, as I briefly mentioned earlier, is if the organisation is satisfied on reasonable grounds that the correction should not be made. This usually happens when the organisation genuinely believes that the personal data it currently holds is factually accurate, and the individual is mistaken or lying. If this happens, the organisation cannot just ignore the individual; they are legally required to annotate the personal data in their system. This annotation must clearly indicate that a correction was requested by the individual on a specific date, but the organisation chose not to make it. The second category involves explicit statutory exceptions to the correction obligation. I will highlight two key exceptions. First, you do not need to correct any "opinion data." This includes professional or expert opinions. The logic here is simple: an opinion, by definition, is a subjective viewpoint and cannot be objectively "wrong" or "incorrect" in the way factual data can. Therefore, you cannot force an organisation to change an opinion. Second, you do not have to correct any "derived personal data." This refers to personal data about an individual that the organisation derived or inferred in the course of business by analysing other personal data. Because the organisation generated this profile or inference internally, it is exempt from mandatory user correction.

    在纠正义务方面，有一项关于成本的非常严格的规定：组织绝对不被允许收取履行纠正请求的费用。请记住，您可以为访问请求收取合理的增量费用，但您不能为修正您自己的记录收费。如果组织同意纠正个人数据，必须在收到请求后切实可行的情况下尽快进行。与访问义务类似，一般监管指南规定的截止日期是30天。如果出于任何原因，您无法在30天内完成纠正，您需要以书面形式通知该个人，并明确说明您何时能够完成。那么，组织在什么时候可以拒绝纠正请求呢？基本上有两大类原因。正如我之前简要提到的，第一类是组织有合理的理由确信不应进行纠正。这通常发生在组织真诚地认为其目前持有的个人数据在事实上是准确的，而个人弄错了或在撒谎时。如果发生这种情况，组织不能仅仅无视该个人；法律要求他们必须在其系统中对该个人数据进行“批注”（annotate）。该批注必须清楚地表明，个人在特定日期要求进行纠正，但组织选择不进行纠正。第二类涉及纠正义务的明确法定例外情况。我将重点介绍两个关键的例外。首先，您不需要纠正任何“意见数据”（opinion data）。这包括专业或专家意见。这里的逻辑很简单：顾名思义，意见是一种主观观点，不能像事实数据那样客观上存在“错”或“不正确”。因此，您不能强迫组织改变一种意见。其次，您不需要纠正任何“衍生个人数据”（derived personal data）。这指的是组织在业务过程中通过分析其他个人数据而推导或推断出的关于个人的数据。由于组织在内部生成了这种画像或推断，因此它可以豁免于用户的强制纠正。

  - [19:47:07 - 19:53:53] **International Comparisons and Student Q&A on Airline Fees**

    Let me draw some quick comparisons with other jurisdictions to show you how common this is. The right to correct your personal data is a very standard privacy right globally. In the EU, Article 16 of the GDPR refers to this as the "right to rectification," stating that the data subject has the right to obtain from the controller the rectification of inaccurate personal data concerning him or her without undue delay. Similarly, in China, Article 46 of the PIPL (Personal Information Protection Law) dictates that if an individual finds their personal information is inaccurate or incomplete, they have the right to request the personal information processor to make corrections or supplements.

    Any questions on the core mechanics?

    *Student:* "Why can airlines charge a fee for a name change? Like, if you enter your name incorrectly when buying a ticket, and you want to correct it, they hit you with a massive change fee. Why can't you just say it's a correction of personal data, which is supposed to be free?"

    *Professor:* That is a very interesting observation. Let's unpack that. Under the PDPA, an organisation cannot charge you a fee if you are merely requesting that the personal data held in their overarching database or user profile be updated. However, what you are requesting in the airline scenario is slightly different. You are not just asking them to update their internal address book; you are explicitly requesting that the name attached to a specific, legally binding contract—the flight ticket—be amended. That modification to the issued contract is what incurs a fee, reflecting their relationship with ticketing systems, not the basic update of a database record.

    *Student:* "But couldn't it be argued that it is still fundamentally your personal data? I'm just correcting a typo in my name. I'm not trying to transfer the ticket to a completely different person. Why should the PDPA's 'no fee' rule not protect this?"

    *Professor:* Technically speaking, you are correct that your name is your personal data. However, airlines argue that changing the name on an issued ticket crosses the line from a simple "data correction" into a "contractual modification." If they didn't charge a fee or restrict this, the system would be heavily exploited.

    *Student:* "Yeah, I'm not from the airline industry, but I think the reason is that air tickets are sold at very different prices depending on when you buy them. Travel operators or agencies could just buy a bunch of cheap tickets under fake or random names, and then 'correct' the data to the real names of their clients later. It's a deterrent to stop ticket scalping."

    *Professor:* Exactly, I would completely agree with that rationale if the person is trying to change the entire identity of the passenger from Person A to Person B. That is clearly a transfer of service, not a data correction. The frustration arises when it is genuinely just a minor typo—fixing one letter in a name. In those cases, applying a massive fee feels like pure exploitation rather than data protection compliance. Unfortunately, airlines have very good lawyers who successfully frame any ticket alteration as a contractual service change rather than a PDPA correction request. It is an intricate workaround, but that is how they justify the fee.

    让我快速与其他司法管辖区做一些比较，向你们展示这是多么普遍。纠正个人数据的权利在全球是一项非常标准的隐私权。在欧盟，GDPR第16条将其称为“更正权”（right to rectification），规定数据主体有权要求控制者毫不迟延地更正与其有关的不准确的个人数据。同样，在中国，《个人信息保护法》（PIPL）第46条规定，如果个人发现其个人信息不准确或不完整，有权请求个人信息处理者更正、补充。

    关于核心机制还有什么问题吗？

    *学生：*“为什么航空公司可以对更改姓名收费？比如，如果您在买票时把名字输入错了，想要纠正，他们会向您收取高额的更改费。为什么您不能直接说这是个人数据的纠正，而这本来应该是免费的？”

    *教授：*这是一个非常有趣的观察。我们来剖析一下。根据PDPA，如果您仅仅是要求更新他们的大型数据库或用户档案中保存的个人数据，组织不能向您收费。但是，在航空公司的场景中，您所要求的略有不同。您不仅仅是要求他们更新内部的通讯录；您是在明确要求修改附属于一份具有法律约束力的特定合同（机票）上的姓名。对已签发合同的修改才是产生费用的原因，这反映了他们与票务系统的关系，而不是对数据库记录的基本更新。

    *学生：*“但这难道不能说它从根本上仍然是你的个人数据吗？我只是在纠正我名字里的一个错别字。我并不是想把票转让给一个完全不同的人。为什么PDPA的‘不收费’规则不能保护这一点呢？”

    *教授：*从技术上讲，您说得对，您的姓名就是您的个人数据。然而，航空公司争辩说，更改已签发机票上的姓名已经跨越了简单的“数据纠正”界限，变成了“合同修改”。如果他们不收费或不对此加以限制，这个系统就会被严重利用。

    *学生：*“对，我虽然不是航空业的，但我认为原因是机票的价格会根据您购买的时间而有很大差异。旅行社或代理机构完全可以用假名或随便起的名字买一堆便宜的票，然后稍后再将数据‘纠正’为他们客户的真实姓名。这是一种防止倒卖机票的威慑手段。”

    *教授：*完全正确，如果这个人试图将乘客的整个身份从A换成B，我完全同意这个理由。这显然是服务的转让，而不是数据纠正。让人沮丧的是，当它真的只是一个小小的拼写错误——修改名字里的一个字母时。在这些情况下，收取高额费用感觉纯粹是剥削，而不是为了遵守数据保护。不幸的是，航空公司有非常优秀的律师，他们成功地将任何机票更改定性为合同服务变更，而不是PDPA下的纠正请求。这是一种复杂的变通方法，但他们就是这样为收费辩护的。

  - [19:53:53 - 20:10:40] **Contested Truths and Complex Correction Scenarios**

    I want to bring up a particularly fascinating case from the EU which illustrates an important point: the correction obligation can create massive administrative headaches when "the truth" itself is contested. The case is C-247/23 VP, decided by the CJEU (Court of Justice of the European Union). The individual, VP, was an Iranian national who obtained refugee status in Hungary. VP was born female but identified as male. As part of the refugee application process, VP produced medical certificates proving they were transgender. Nevertheless, the Hungarian authorities registered VP's sex as female in the official asylum register based on birth biology. VP subsequently submitted a formal data rectification request to have their gender identity corrected to male in the register. The Hungarian authorities rejected this request, stating that VP had not provided medical proof of having undergone gender reassignment surgery. They argued that without the surgery, the registration of "female" was factually accurate. The case went all the way to the CJEU, which ultimately sided with the individual. The court ruled that authorities must rectify a public register if the gender identity of the individual is inaccurate, and crucially, they stated that authorities cannot demand evidence of gender reassignment surgery as a strict condition for rectifying that data. It highlights how definitions of fundamental characteristics—like male or female—can lead to severe problems when someone is asking to have their personal data corrected.

    Can you all think of other potential situations where a similar difficulty involving a "contested truth" might arise?

    *Student 1:* "I can think of religion. If your company record says Buddhism, but years later you convert to Christianity and ask HR to update it. Do you need to get a baptism certificate to prove the change, or do they just take your word for it?"

    *Student 2:* "Mental health records in a company. If you were previously supported for a mental health condition, but later want that removed from your internal record because you feel you are fully recovered. The company doctor might refuse to change it."

    *Student 3:* "Origin or race, especially regarding refugees claiming Tamil origin. State definitions of race might heavily differ from unofficial or personal concepts of race."

    *Student 4:* "I have a friend who runs an event agency. A client told him he was divorced and demanded his marital status be updated, but refused to provide the divorce papers for privacy reasons. The agency refused to change it."

    *Student 5:* "What if a couple is undergoing a divorce and they want their status listed as 'separated', but the organisation's database dropdown only has 'married' or 'divorced'?"

    *Student 6:* "Parentage. Biological parents versus adoptive parents. Who legally counts as the 'parent' in a basic form?"

    *Student 7:* "Criminal or disciplinary records. If you were charged but later cleared, can an organisation refuse to delete the record by arguing that it is a historical fact that you *were* investigated?"

    *Professor:* These are all fantastic examples of how messy this gets in practice. When dealing with characteristics where the truth can be contested—whether it's race, marital status, or criminal history—the burden often falls on the concept of "reasonable grounds." In Singapore, we are unlikely to have massive constitutional problems with this under the PDPA because the PDPA solely regulates the private sector; public government agencies and official state registries are completely exempt from it. However, in a private sector setting like the event agency example, if an individual refuses to provide evidence (like a divorce certificate) for a contested change, the organisation can easily rely on the statutory exception. The organisation would formally state: "We are satisfied on reasonable grounds that the correction should not be made because you have failed to provide the necessary supporting documentation." Similarly, for the dropdown menu issue, if the system technically cannot reflect "separated," the data is legally considered accurate as "married" until the divorce is finalised. For criminal records, it depends entirely on how the specific question was phrased on the form. If the form asked "Have you ever been investigated?", and you were cleared later, the factual answer remains "Yes." The truth is only contested if the question is vague.

    我想提一个来自欧盟的特别引人入胜的案例，它说明了一个重要观点：当“真相”本身存在争议时，纠正义务可能会带来巨大的行政管理难题。这个案例是欧洲法院（CJEU）裁决的C-247/23 VP案。当事人VP是一名在匈牙利获得难民身份的伊朗国民。VP出生时是女性，但自我认同为男性。作为难民申请程序的一部分，VP出示了证明其为跨性别者的医疗证明。然而，匈牙利当局基于出生时的生理特征，在官方难民登记册中将VP的性别登记为女性。随后，VP提交了一份正式的数据更正请求，要求在登记册中将其性别认同更正为男性。匈牙利当局拒绝了这一请求，指出VP没有提供已经接受性别重置手术的医疗证明。他们争辩说，如果没有进行手术，登记为“女性”在事实上就是准确的。这个案子一直打到了欧洲法院，法院最终支持了该个人。法院裁定，如果个人的性别认同不准确，当局必须更正公共登记册；最关键的是，他们指出，当局不能将提供性别重置手术证据作为更正该数据的严格条件。这突显了在有人要求纠正其个人数据时，对基本特征（如男性或女性）的定义可能会导致严重的问题。

    大家能想到其他可能出现类似“有争议的真相”的潜在情况吗？

    *学生1：*“我能想到宗教信仰。如果你的公司记录上写着佛教，但几年后你改信了基督教，并要求人力资源部更新。你需要获得洗礼证书来证明这种改变吗，还是他们直接相信你的话？”

    *学生2：*“公司里的心理健康记录。如果你之前因为心理健康状况接受过援助，但后来你觉得自己完全康复了，想从内部记录中删除它。公司医生可能会拒绝更改。”

    *学生3：*“出身或种族，特别是关于声称是泰米尔血统的难民。国家对种族的定义可能与非官方或个人的种族概念有很大差异。”

    *学生6：*“父母身份。亲生父母与养父母。在一份基本表格中，谁在法律上算作‘父母’？”

    *教授：*这些都是极好的例子，说明了在实践中这会有多麻烦。在处理真相可能存在争议的特征时——无论是种族、婚姻状况还是犯罪史——重担通常落在“合理理由”这个概念上。在新加坡，我们在PDPA下不太可能遇到大规模的宪法问题，因为PDPA只监管私营部门；公共政府机构和官方国家登记处完全豁免于该法案。然而，在像活动策划公司例子那样的私营部门环境中，如果个人拒绝为有争议的更改提供证据（如离婚证），组织可以很容易地依赖法定例外。组织会正式声明：“我们有合理的理由确信不应进行纠正，因为您未能提供必要的证明文件。”同样，对于下拉菜单的问题，如果系统在技术上无法反映“分居”，那么在离婚手续最终完成之前，数据在法律上被认为是准确的“已婚”。对于犯罪记录，这完全取决于表格上具体问题的表述方式。如果表格问的是“您是否曾被调查过？”，即使您后来被洗清了嫌疑，事实上的答案仍然是“是”。只有当问题模棱两可时，真相才会产生争议。

  - [20:10:40 - 20:17:40] **The Right to Erasure and the Shoppa Case Study**

    I want to highlight one more point of comparison regarding a right that actually does *not* exist in Singapore: the right to erasure, commonly known as the "right to be forgotten." In some jurisdictions, the EU being the undisputed leader, you not only have the right to correct your personal data, but you also have the explicit right to have it completely erased or made extremely hard to find. Under Article 17 of the EU GDPR, the data subject has a right to obtain the erasure of personal data without undue delay under certain circumstances. Similarly, Section 12 of India's DPDPA grants the right to erasure. The principal landmark case that established this concept globally is the Google Spain case (Case C-131/12). The individual involved was Mario Costeja Gonzalez, a Spanish resident. He realised that whenever he typed his full name into Google Search, the first results were links to old pages of the *La Vanguardia* newspaper. These pages contained a legal announcement mentioning his name in relation to a real-estate auction initiated for the recovery of social security debts. He had long since paid off these debts and resolved the proceedings, so he felt this old, damaging news was completely irrelevant to his current life. He asked the Spanish courts to force the newspaper to remove the articles, but the court rejected this, citing freedom of expression for the original publisher. However, Gonzalez also asked Google to block those specific links from appearing when people searched his name. The CJEU ruled in his favour against Google. The court established that a data subject has the right to request that search engines remove links to certain information from search results to prevent it from being made readily available to the public. The court declared that the privacy rights of the individual override the economic interests of the search engine, and even the general public's interest in accessing that data, unless the individual is a prominent public figure where the public has an overriding interest in knowing their past. It is a fascinating area of law, but remember, this right to be forgotten does not apply under Singapore's PDPA.

    Finally, I want to leave you with a hypothetical question regarding high-content technology to think about over the break. Look at the slide regarding Company PQR. PQR operates an e-commerce site called Shoppa. Shoppa automatically personalises search results for its users by creating detailed profiles based on their interactions, searches, and purchases. This profiling is done entirely with the informed consent of all users, so there is no consent issue here. We have a user named Lenny. Lenny is frustrated because whenever he searches for items, Shoppa only shows him things on the absolute highest end of the price range. Wondering what is going on, Lenny submits an access request to view his algorithmically generated profile. How should PQR respond to this access request? Let's assume PQR grants access, and Lenny discovers a specific data point in his profile that predicts his monthly income is between 20,000 and 30,000 dollars. In reality, his income is only 4,000 dollars. Lenny submits a correction request asking Shoppa to fix this income bracket, but he absolutely refuses to provide Shoppa with his actual payslips to prove his income, citing privacy concerns. The question is: How should PQR respond to this specific correction request, and can they legally refuse to change the algorithm's prediction?

    Think about how the specific wording of the PDPA provisions we just covered applies here. We will take a short break now and come back at 8:25 to discuss the answer.

    [Image: Diagram comparing the 'Right to Correction' (updating an inaccurate income profile on an e-commerce site) vs the 'Right to Erasure' (removing old, damaging search engine links as seen in the Google Spain case).]

    我想再强调一个比较点，关于一项在新加坡实际上“并不存在”的权利：删除权（right to erasure），通常被称为“被遗忘权”（right to be forgotten）。在一些司法管辖区，欧盟是毫无争议的领导者，你不仅有权纠正你的个人数据，还有明确的权利要求将其完全删除或使其极难被找到。根据欧盟GDPR第17条，在某些情况下，数据主体有权要求毫不迟延地删除个人数据。同样，印度DPDPA第12条也赋予了删除权。在全球范围内确立这一概念的主要里程碑案件是Google Spain案（C-131/12案）。涉案当事人是西班牙居民Mario Costeja Gonzalez。他发现，每当他在谷歌搜索中输入自己的全名时，最先出现的结果是《先锋报》（La Vanguardia）旧网页的链接。这些网页包含一份法律公告，其中提到了他的名字，内容是关于为追讨社会保障债务而发起的房地产拍卖。他早就还清了这些债务并解决了诉讼，所以他觉得这些具有破坏性的旧新闻与他现在的生活完全无关。他要求西班牙法院强迫该报纸删除这些文章，但法院以原出版商的言论自由为由拒绝了。然而，Gonzalez还要求谷歌在人们搜索他的名字时，屏蔽这些特定链接的出现。欧洲法院（CJEU）作出了有利于他、反对谷歌的裁决。法院确立了数据主体有权要求搜索引擎从搜索结果中删除指向某些信息的链接，以防止公众轻易获取这些信息。法院宣布，个人的隐私权凌驾于搜索引擎的经济利益之上，甚至凌驾于公众获取该数据的普遍利益之上，除非该个人是著名的公众人物，公众对了解其过去有压倒性的利益。这是一个引人入胜的法律领域，但请记住，这项被遗忘权在新加坡的PDPA下并不适用。

    最后，我想给大家留一个关于高内容技术的假设性问题，让大家在休息时思考一下。请看关于PQR公司的幻灯片。PQR运营着一个名为Shoppa的电子商务网站。Shoppa通过基于用户的互动、搜索和购买记录创建详细的个人档案，自动为用户个性化搜索结果。这种用户画像完全是在所有用户知情同意的情况下进行的，因此这里不存在同意的问题。我们有一个叫Lenny的用户。Lenny很沮丧，因为每当他搜索商品时，Shoppa只向他展示价格范围内绝对最高端的商品。Lenny想知道发生了什么事，于是提交了一份访问请求，要求查看他由算法生成的个人档案。PQR应该如何回应这个访问请求？假设PQR授予了访问权限，Lenny发现他的个人档案中有一个特定的数据点，预测他的月收入在20,000到30,000美元之间。实际上，他的收入只有4,000美元。Lenny提交了一份纠正请求，要求Shoppa修正这个收入区间，但他以隐私为由，断然拒绝向Shoppa提供他的实际工资单来证明他的收入。问题是：PQR应该如何回应这个具体的纠正请求？他们在法律上能拒绝更改算法的预测吗？

    想一想我们刚刚讲过的PDPA条款的具体措辞在这里是如何适用的。我们现在短暂休息一下，8点25分回来讨论答案。

  - [20:28:49 - 20:39:15] **Case Study Discussion: E-commerce Profiling and the Correction Obligation**

    Let us discuss the hypothetical case of Company PQR and their e-commerce site, Shoppa. We have Lenny, who wants access to his algorithmically generated profile because he believes it is unfairly showing him expensive items. The first question is whether PQR can refuse his access request. You could try to argue that the algorithm's profile is "opinion data" kept for an "evaluative purpose," but if you look at the strict statutory list of evaluative purposes—like determining suitability for employment or insurance—customising retail search results simply does not fit. Therefore, you cannot rely on the evaluative purpose exception to deny access. Assuming PQR provides access, Lenny then sees a predicted income of 20,000 to 30,000 dollars and demands it be corrected to his actual income of 4,000 dollars, but he refuses to provide proof. Can Shoppa refuse this correction request? There are two primary grounds for refusal here. First, a student correctly pointed out that Shoppa can rely on the "derived personal data" exception. The income bracket is not raw data Lenny provided; it is a prediction derived internally from his browsing and purchasing interactions on the site, so the company is not obligated to correct it. Second, Shoppa can state that they are "satisfied on reasonable grounds that the correction should not be made" specifically because Lenny refused to provide any proof of his real income. While these are strong legal defences, a student also brought up the customer service perspective. Practically speaking, fighting a customer over this is bad business. A Data Protection Officer (DPO) might tell the sales manager they have the legal right to refuse, but the business side will likely just choose to correct the data to retain the customer. Additionally, a student asked about the extent of the access obligation—do you have to explain the underlying algorithm? The answer is no; strictly speaking, the access obligation only requires you to produce the personal data (the profile itself), not to justify the mathematical weights of the algorithm. Finally, someone brought up price discrimination, which is a very real issue here, but that is a separate moral and business question outside the strict confines of the PDPA access and correction obligations.

    [Image: Flowchart showing the decision-making process for Access and Correction requests involving derived algorithmic profiling data, highlighting statutory exceptions.]

    让我们来讨论一下PQR公司及其电子商务网站Shoppa的假设案例。我们有一位名叫Lenny的用户，他希望访问自己由算法生成的个人档案，因为他认为该网站不公平地只向他展示昂贵的商品。第一个问题是，PQR能否拒绝他的访问请求？你可以尝试争辩说，算法生成的档案是为了“评估目的”而保留的“意见数据”。但是，如果你查看法定的评估目的清单（如确定就业或保险资格），定制零售搜索结果根本不符合其中的规定。因此，你不能依赖评估目的例外来拒绝访问。假设PQR提供了访问权限，Lenny随后看到预测的收入在20,000到30,000美元之间，并要求将其更正为他4,000美元的实际收入，但他拒绝提供证据。Shoppa能拒绝这个纠正请求吗？这里有两个主要的拒绝理由。首先，一位学生正确地指出，Shoppa可以依赖“衍生个人数据”例外。这个收入区间不是Lenny提供的原始数据；它是公司根据他在网站上的浏览和购买互动在内部推导出的预测，因此公司没有义务纠正它。其次，Shoppa可以声明他们“有合理的理由确信不应进行纠正”，具体原因就是Lenny拒绝提供任何关于其真实收入的证明。虽然这些是强有力的法律抗辩，但也有学生从客户服务的角度提出了看法。实际上，在这件事上与客户作对是不利于业务的。数据保护官（DPO）可能会告诉销售经理，他们在法律上有权拒绝，但业务部门很可能只会选择纠正数据以留住客户。此外，一位学生问到了访问义务的范围——你必须解释底层的算法吗？答案是否定的；严格来说，访问义务只要求你提供个人数据（个人档案本身），而不需要证明算法中数学权重的合理性。最后，有人提到了价格歧视，这在这里确实是一个非常现实的问题，但那是一个独立的道德和商业问题，超出了PDPA访问和纠正义务的严格范畴。

  - [20:39:15 - 20:44:09] **Introduction to the Accuracy Obligation**

    Let me quickly move on to the next major topic for today, which is the Accuracy Obligation. The Accuracy Obligation requires organisations to make a "reasonable effort" to ensure that the personal data they have collected is accurate and complete. You do not have to guarantee this all the time, but the obligation is strictly triggered under two specific circumstances: first, if the personal data is likely to be used by the organisation to make a decision that affects the individual, and second, if the personal data is likely to be disclosed to another organisation. If either of these is true, you must try to ensure the data is accurate. Note that the legal wording is "reasonable effort," meaning you do not have to guarantee perfect accuracy. A student asked if an organisation has to explicitly document or keep track of all its verification steps in a specific written form. The PDPA does not prescribe exactly how you must comply; it is up to the organisation to design an internal system—whether that involves checking data right before a decision is made or running an annual audit. What counts as a reasonable effort depends on several factors: the nature and sensitivity of the data, the purpose of its use, the reliability of the source, the currency (or age) of the data, and most importantly, the severity of the impact on the individual if the data is inaccurate. There are a few practical rules of thumb you can lean on. If the individual provided the personal data themselves, the organisation can generally presume it is accurate at the time of collection. For example, if Nick applies for a credit card and provides his current income and payslip, the bank can assume it is correct and has met its reasonable effort threshold. However, you still need to check the currency of the data later on. If, two years later, Nick applies for a home loan, the bank cannot just rely on that old data to make such a significant financial decision; reasonable effort dictates they must ask for fresh documentation. Conversely, if the personal data was provided by a third party rather than the individual, the organisation needs to be much more careful. For instance, if an adventure camp operator receives an employee's health check-up records from their company, but those records are eight years old, the camp operator must take proactive steps to verify the data's current accuracy, usually by asking the individual to confirm it.

    让我快速进入今天的下一个主要话题：准确性义务（Accuracy Obligation）。准确性义务要求组织做出“合理努力”，以确保其收集的个人数据是准确和完整的。你不需要在任何时候都保证这一点，但该义务在两种特定情况下会被严格触发：第一，如果该个人数据可能被组织用于做出影响该个人的决定；第二，如果该个人数据可能被披露给另一个组织。如果符合其中任何一种情况，你就必须设法确保数据的准确性。请注意，法律上的措辞是“合理努力”，这意味着你不需要保证绝对的完美准确。有学生问，组织是否必须以特定的书面形式明确记录或追踪其所有的验证步骤。PDPA并没有确切规定你必须如何遵守；这取决于组织自己设计内部系统——无论是在做出决定之前检查数据，还是进行年度审计。什么算作合理努力取决于几个因素：数据的性质和敏感性、使用的目的、来源的可靠性、数据的时效性（或年份），以及最重要的一点，如果数据不准确，对个人造成影响的严重程度。这里有一些实用的经验法则可以依靠。如果个人数据是个人自己提供的，组织通常可以假定它在收集时是准确的。例如，如果Nick申请信用卡并提供他目前的收入和工资单，银行可以认为这是准确的，并且已经达到了合理努力的门槛。然而，你以后仍然需要检查数据的时效性。如果两年后，Nick申请房屋贷款，银行不能仅仅依靠那些旧数据来做出如此重大的财务决定；合理努力要求他们必须索要最新的证明文件。相反，如果个人数据是由第三方而不是个人提供的，组织就需要更加小心。例如，如果一家探险营地运营商从某公司收到了其员工的健康检查记录，但这些记录是八年前的，营地运营商就必须采取积极措施来验证数据当前的准确性，通常的做法是要求该个人进行确认。

  - [20:44:09 - 20:50:32] **Case Study: Credit Bureau (Singapore) Pte Ltd and the 'HX' Rating**
